Indian Prime Minister Mr. Narendra Modi launches IIT Madras mission to create 3D atlases of 100+ Human Brains

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sudha Gopalakrishnan Brain Centre at IIT Madras, India's No. 1-ranked engineering institute, aims to create the world's largest set of whole-human-brain 3D maps at cellular resolution, advancing research into brain structure, development and disease

CHENNAI, India, Nov. 18, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Hon'ble Indian Prime Minister Mr. Narendra Modi launched a path breaking brain research at India's No. 1-ranked engineering institute, the Indian Institute of Technology Madras (IIT Madras).

Mr. Narendra Modi on 11th October 2026 launched IIT Madras Sudha Gopalakrishnan Brain Centre's (SGBC) scientific mission to create 3D atlases of 100+ human brains at cellular resolution, spanning the human lifespan from foetal stage to old age, and including brain-related diseases.

The mission aims to create the world's largest collection of whole-human-brain 3D maps at cellular resolution, advancing research into brain structure, development, and disease.

The mission uses SGBC's advanced imaging, computing and data visualisation technologies to transform whole post-mortem human brains into detailed digital maps. These resources will be publicly accessible, enabling researchers and medical professionals worldwide to explore human brain structure in unprecedented detail.

Mr. Kris Gopalakrishnan, IIT Madras Distinguished Alumnus, Infosys Co-founder and a major donor, said, "The Prime Minister's visit would inspire scientists and encourage philanthropists to support and scale up world-class research in India."

Prof. Mohanasankar Sivaprakasam, Head of SGBC, IIT Madras, said, "The initiative brings together brain science, engineering, medicine and technology, with the potential to shape neuroscience and neuromedicine for decades."

At the launch, the Hon'ble Prime Minister also released C-STROKE, the world's first 3D atlas of an ischemic stroke-affected human brain. Supported by IIT Madras Distinguished Alumnus Mr. Prem Watsa, Chairman of Fairfax Financial, the atlas connects clinical Magnetic Resonance Imaging (MRI) scans with microscopic examination of brain tissue.

Featuring over 1000 annotated brain sections, C-STROKE maps cellular indicators of neuronal health, neuroinflammation, blood-brain barrier disruption, oxygen deprivation and axonal injury. Its open-access visualisation and analytical tools will help researchers investigate stroke pathology and identify potential therapeutic targets.

The Centre has already released atlases of second trimester fetal brain development 'DHARANI' (2025), and brain stem of fetus, child, adult 'ANCHOR' (2026).

The broader mission brings together more than 200 researchers, engineers and technicians at SGBC, over 10 Indian medical institutions, and over 30 leading researchers from over 10 countries. The Centre is currently supported by over 20 industries and philanthropies.

→ Baca artikel asli

The smartest AI might be the one that leaves us alone

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

One of the ideas I’m always trying to tell my students is that their brains are something, an asset, if you like, they need to protect. The human brain is an amazing optimizer: Provide it with an effortless way to achieve something, and it will quickly start to use it. We used to memorize a ton of phone numbers a few decades ago. Now we can hardly memorize our own, simply because our brain knows the number is a few clicks away. 

A clear example is why I never use AI to write my articles: simply because, for me, in my personal methodology as a professor of innovation, writing is my way of making concepts stick in my brain, concepts I will need later to teach and discuss. If I ask ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini, or any other chatbot to write my articles for me, I would lose much of the learning that writing gives me.

This doesn’t mean I don’t use AI: I can use it to translate certain parts, to search for relevant articles to back up an assertion, or to assess whether the concepts I try to explain come across clearly. But I don’t want to use it for writing, because writing is a skill I want to protect. And like a muscle, if you don’t exercise it, it quickly atrophies.

For a manager, the skill worth protecting might be decision-making: An AI that makes every choice could undermine precisely the judgment that person needs to develop. The useful behavior depends on the objective, and sometimes that objective requires the system to hold back. 

What happens when we move from personal to corporate use? Can an AI learn when its intervention would make things worse? That capability might receive very little applause in a product demonstration, but it could be enormously valuable in practice.

The sales agent that lets us be 

Imagine a company using a sales agent. When it identifies a customer approaching renewal, it follows a standard procedure: It quickly reviews the account, writes a personalized email, offers a discount, and schedules a follow-up. All of it in mere seconds. And the audience claps enthusiastically. 

But what if our customer would have renewed anyway? Then, the end result is that we have wasted an interaction, risked alienating the customer, and surrendered margin unnecessarily. What if our customer is a happy one, and the right thing to do would have been just to wait for him or her to renew voluntarily? 

Even when all tasks are perfectly executed, the decision to perform them could have been perfectly wrong. 

The cost of being helpful

We know how this feels when we’re working with real people: Experienced employees often develop a sense of when to follow up, when to escalate an issue, and when making too many interventions will look messy, send the wrong signal, or make the customer upset. How do they know that? It comes with experience; it’s learning that compounds over time, with more and more iterations.

That judgment is something we pay to have. However, when we evaluate AI, we tend to be fascinated with visible activity—with messages generated, tasks completed, workflows executed, etc. The equivalent is if we were dealing with a hyperactive employee. And as we probably know, someone who sends twice the number of messages is probably not twice as valuable. 

In other words, just because an action can be automated and, therefore, performed easily, doesn’t mean that we should be performing it all the time. 

Look what happens with AI coding assistants: In “When to show a suggestion? Integrating human feedback in AI-assisted programming,” Microsoft researchers used data from 535 programmers to retrospectively evaluate a system that selectively withheld suggestions that were apparently likely to be rejected by the developers. They also found that optimizing for suggestion acceptance could produce lower-quality suggestions. Getting people to accept more assistance was an imperfect measure of helping them. 

Learning when to intervene

In the case of our renewal agent, a system that learns from a lot of comparable cases could perhaps investigate which interventions help with whom. For instance, some customers don’t like the feeling of being “directed” or “influenced,” and are better left to their own devices. Some like a particular type of interaction through a particular channel, but they hate others. Or they want to talk to the same customer representatives every time because they remember them. 

The function to protect would be the ability to spot these cases. Do not automate; optimize. But optimization is not necessarily something that today’s corporate AI does well. Today’s corporate AIs can generate splendid emails, but following the before and after and deciding to do nothing is usually not an alternative. 

It’s the experience from all previous cases, processed along with the specific characteristics of every customer, that leads to the lessons learned, to the continuous improvement, to the process being optimized. And that ability to improve decisions through accumulated experience is precisely what you need. 

Give waiting a fair chance

When buying or deploying AI, managers should, therefore, find out what outcome the system is theoretically trying to improve, and design ways to know whether its actions helped or not. We need to be honest here: A renewal following an email does not necessarily establish that the email was the reason for the renewal. Customers differ, circumstances change, and useful comparisons may require carefully designed experiments. Simply storing more interaction histories will not settle the question. 

This includes maintaining the necessary human oversight, the now-famous “human-in-the-loop”: A missed warning or an unanswered complaint can be costly. People must define the obligations and limits within which the system can learn. 

I haven’t seen an AI system that reviews an account, checks all the available evidence from the corporate ERP or CRM, and decides that contacting the customer would probably make things worse. So the final outcome would be that nothing gets sent, no discount is offered, and we just let the customer evolve naturally. No hyperactivity. Why? Because our corporate learning optimized it, and the accumulated learning behind that decision should remain under our company’s control, available even if we replace the model or the vendor powering our agents.

It might be a disappointing demo. But it could be excellent business.

InfoQ Homepage News AWS PrivateLink Tunnel Endpoints Open a Whole CIDR Range to Another Account

Nov 18, 2026 4 min read

AWS has published a technical walkthrough of tunnel endpoints, a VPC endpoint type launched in September that lets an application in one VPC reach every address in a CIDR range shared from another account, instead of one resource at a time. The provider shares a CIDR-type resource configuration through AWS Resource Access Manager and the consumer creates a tunnel endpoint against it. From then on, anything inside the range is reachable, including resources that did not exist when the share was made.

Bruce Pinsky and Ashish Kumar of AWS frame it as a vendor-access problem, for "a vendor or service provider whose software must reach applications and resources in a customer's network." Until now, that meant a separate PrivateLink resource configuration per database or host, which breaks down when the resources are ephemeral, or the customer does not want to enumerate them. With a CIDR configuration, the customer defines a perimeter in address ranges, and "resources located outside the perimeter can't be reached by the vendor." Two further properties come with the design: the consumer and provider VPCs can use overlapping CIDR blocks, and connections "can start only from the consumer VPC, not from the provider network."

(Source: AWS Networking & Content Delivery blog)

On the r/aws thread about the September launch, the first question was what this does that VPC peering does not, from a self-described veteran of AWS's advanced networking components. The answers that followed are the use cases: a peering connection exposes the whole VPC in both directions, while a tunnel endpoint shares a subset of the address space in one direction only; one commenter at a bank wrote that "peering with unregulated partners is a massive no-no, the auditors will rip you to shreds for it." Another pointed to clusters that do client-side load balancing, such as self-managed Kafka or Aerospike, which until now needed port-based routing on a Network Load Balancer and a separate port-mapping horizon for connections arriving over an interface endpoint. The same thread drew the line against VPC Lattice itself: Lattice services work at layer 7, tunnel endpoints at layer 4 without a resource configuration per target.

The unusual part is on the consumer side: the application's traffic has to arrive at the tunnel endpoint wrapped in GENEVE, the encapsulation protocol defined in RFC 8926, over UDP port 6081 and in the same Availability Zone. The endpoint strips the header, checks that the inner destination falls inside the shared range, rewrites the source to a resource gateway address, and forwards a plain packet into the provider VPC. Linux can do the wrapping in the kernel. The walkthrough creates an ip link add ... type geneve id 0 device inside its own network namespace with a default route through it, which sends the provider's ranges into the tunnel and leaves the instance's other traffic alone. On the far side, the resources see the resource gateway as the client, so the post advises security groups that reference the gateway's security group or subnet rather than a single address.

The documentation lists the constraints. Inner traffic must be TCP, with UDP permitted only for DNS, so ICMP and ping do not work, and IP fragments are dropped. Private DNS names are not supported on tunnel endpoints. The endpoint's subnets must sit in Availability Zones that overlap with the resource gateway's, or the association shows as inaccessible. A resource gateway's DNS setting cannot be changed after creation. And the access logs identify the consumer's endpoint but carry no field for the inner source address, so a provider can attribute traffic to a vendor and not to a workload behind that vendor's endpoint. The post also warns that sharing 0.0.0.0/0 grants access to everything the provider VPC can route to, including networks behind VPC peering, Direct Connect or a Site-to-Site VPN, and recommends sharing the narrowest ranges that work.

Both points have been tested in practice. Ryo Suzuki of the Japanese consultancy Classmethod connected two VPCs that both use 10.0.0.0/16 and got HTTP through, but only by running a GENEVE relay as root on the client, by routing a /32 per destination host, or by putting an HAProxy or NAT instance in front, each of which adds a hop or a single point of failure; his conclusion was that "route management according to the number of destinations is required." In a second experiment, he shared 0.0.0.0/0 from a VPC with a NAT gateway and reached the internet from a VPC with no gateway at all, which confirmed AWS's warning from the other side; he judged it workable as a temporary path and "difficult to use in a permanent production setup."

Pricing is an hourly charge per tunnel endpoint plus a per-GB charge for data processed, under PrivateLink pricing, with the provider side billed per GB through the resource gateway under VPC Lattice pricing, and the feature is available in 28 regions. Terraform support followed from the community: a contributor request opened two days after launch was implemented by another contributor and shipped in version 6.71.0 of the AWS provider, as a Tunnel endpoint type on aws_vpc_endpoint and a cidr_resource block on the Lattice resource configuration.

At Axios’ BFD summit, he said much of the pushback against AI is manufactured but still real.

Donald Trump Jr. speaking at AmericaFest at the Phoenix Convention Center in Phoenix, Arizona, on 21 December 2025.

Donald Trump Jr has signalled that investors underestimated the political backlash against AI, Axios reported on Tuesday. He spoke to Axios Pro Rata author Dan Primack at the outlet’s BFD summit in New York.

Primack asked whether investors, or Trump Jr himself, had underestimated the populist pushback. Trump Jr said he thought they had seen that very clearly.

He added that a lot of the opposition is manufactured, but said that does not make it any less real, according to Axios. He also suggested that the founders of the biggest AI companies are not the best people to make the industry’s case.

Trump Jr is a partner at venture firm 1789 Capital, and appeared alongside its co-founder Omeed Malik. The firm was leading a $1bn funding round for prediction market Polymarket, Axios reported in September.

His remarks follow this month’s US midterm elections, which many saw as a rebuke of the AI industry. Voters rejected data centre plans in all but one of 41 ballot measures on the issue, Al Jazeera reported.

Trump Jr agreed with Primack that anger at data centres reflects wider worry about AI’s growing influence, Axios reported.

DBS lets eligible institutions in Asia mint and redeem USDC around the clock

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Building on their Project BLOOM collaboration, Singapore's DBS and Circle are giving eligible institutions direct, 24/7 access to USDC issuance and redemption.

DBS and Circle Circle Internet Group, Inc. (CRCL) are giving eligible institutional clients in Asia direct access to direct USDC minting and redemption through the bank, with settlement between dollars and the stablecoin available around the clock.

In a statement on Wednesday, the Singapore-based bank said the service will help institutions reduce their reliance on secondary market liquidity and extend settlement outside banking hours and cutoff times.

The service targets treasury, settlement, and liquidity management as institutions increase their use of tokenized money, it added.

"We believe the future of financial services will be multi-modal and multi-instrumental, with different forms of tokenized money serving different needs," said Evy Theunis, head of digital assets at DBS Bank's Institutional Banking Group. "By supporting USDC, alongside other trusted tokenised money instruments, we are providing institutions greater choice in how they move and manage value, based on what best serves their needs."

The DBS-Circle partnership also builds on their participation in Project BLOOM, a Monetary Authority of Singapore initiative testing interoperability between forms of tokenized money and financial networks.

Circle's USDC averaged $73.3 billion in circulation during the third quarter, up 8% from a year earlier, according to the company's latest financial results. Onchain transaction volume reached $13.9 trillion, up 49% year over year, though the stablecoin's market share slipped to 27%.

According to The Block's data, USDC is the world's second-largest stablecoin after Tether's USDT, with a supply of $69.3 billion as of Wednesday, down from $75.3 billion at the beginning of the year. USDT's supply has also decreased to $183 billion from about $187 billion over the same period.

The European Banking Authority classified USDC as the first "significant" e-money token under MiCA, with direct oversight of its reserves, liquidity and redemption plans from Nov. 12.

Circle has also expanded the stablecoin's commercial reach this year. Binance bought $100 million of Circle shares in September under a five-year USDC promotion arrangement and the company also signed a Chelsea FC sponsorship deal in August.

On Tuesday, the World Bank Group said it was working with Circle and Citi to test regulated dollar stablecoins for fund disbursements, initially using an intermediated and interoperable model.

Why CoreWeave May Be the Biggest Loser From Jensen Huang's Resignation

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

CoreWeave's partnership with Nvidia is a critical factor in the company's path to success.

As most tech investors are now finding out, the resignation of Nvidia (NVDA -0.44%) CEO Jensen Huang has implications across the industry. Since Nvidia is the dominant company in artificial intelligence accelerators, the industry now operates under a cloud of uncertainty that most investors could not have foreseen just days ago.

Aside from Nvidia itself, neocloud company CoreWeave (CRWV +0.02%) is arguably the most affected. Considering its close relationship with Nvidia, investors may have to rethink that cloud stock's investment case. Here's why.

Nvidia is a major investor in CoreWeave, but that is only the tip of the iceberg in its relationship with the company. Nvidia is also the primary GPU supplier, and CoreWeave has often received early access to Nvidia's latest technology, arguably giving it a competitive advantage over competing neoclouds.

Moreover, Nvidia is CoreWeave's backstop. Nvidia even signed a $6.3 billion agreement to purchase any excess capacity from CoreWeave through 2032. This is critical, as CoreWeave has accumulated nearly $47 billion in debt to built out its operations and fulfill its nearly $133 billion backlog for neocloud infrastructure.

This news comes on the heels of the company's third-quarter 2026 earnings report, which by most accounts was fantastic news for CoreWeave stock investors.

In Q3, revenue rose 168% year over year to nearly $3.7 billion. Also, the company raised guidance on both its revenue and operating income amid strong pricing power. Additionally, it pledged not to issue any additional unsecured debt at current yields through 2027, despite its need for additional infrastructure.

Unfortunately, that news came before Huang resigned, and that departure brings uncertainty to the Nvidia-CoreWeave partnership. Admittedly, Nvidia is unlikely to back out of its contracts with CoreWeave no matter who becomes CEO.

However, until we know who the next permanent CEO will be, we have no idea if or how the relationship will continue. Will Nvidia continue holding its CoreWeave investment? How much will it mean to get Nvidia's updated products first if Huang is no longer driving Nvidia's technical direction?

Until investors have better clarity on these questions, a cautious approach to CoreWeave stock might be prudent.

Given CoreWeave's current situation and the uncertainties surrounding its Nvidia partnership, investors should probably limit their investments to speculative positions.

Admittedly, CoreWeave is growing fast, and the massive backlog could translate into massive growth under the right circumstances. Unfortunately, CoreWeave is also heavily in debt, and the ability to grow the company and service that debt depends heavily on its Nvidia relationship.

Even though that relationship is likely to continue in some form, it is unclear how committed Nvidia will be to the partnership until new management weighs in. Amid such uncertainty, it is likely too risky to hold significant positions in CoreWeave for now.

The three-year agreement with producers’ body Pact still needs approval from Equity’s 8,000 film and TV members.

Producers would only get consent for one specific project, not for any use. Actors must also approve digital changes to scenes with agreed nudity or simulated sex.

Producers could not use a replica during a strike where consent is needed. They must also limit who can see replicas and take care to keep them safe.

World Bank partners with Circle, Citi to explore stablecoins for payments

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The initiative builds on the World Bank’s existing blockchain platform, which aims to create more fund traceability for investments in developing countries.

The World Bank Group said Tuesday it would explore using Circle’s USDC stablecoin to get money to borrowing countries faster and at a lower cost.

The group will work with Citi and Circle to see how payments in stablecoins tied to the US dollar could fit into the banking network it already uses to send money abroad, according to a press release.

Circle would provide the tools for USDC (USDC) issuance and transfers, while Citi would connect the project to existing banking networks. The group plans to start with borrowing countries that allow the use of digital assets.

The World Bank is a development bank owned by its member countries. It lends to governments in low- and middle-income countries and gives grants to some of the poorest, helping fund schools, health clinics and clean water systems.

The bank began rolling out blockchain tool FundsChain in 2025 to let project teams and auditors see where the money goes. The bank says the tool now covers more than 350 projects in 96 countries.

The World Bank in 2023 warned that widespread use of stablecoins could displace local money and make it harder for central banks to manage economies.

In June, CGAP, a research group based at the World Bank, examined using stablecoins to deliver humanitarian aid. The report found that stablecoins can make it easier for an aid agency to move money across borders. However, when recipients are paid directly in stablecoins, they may have to pay to convert them to local currency.

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Abdulrahman Alfozan has pleaded not guilty to intercepting conversations in an executive conference room at OpenAI’s San Francisco offices.

Aformer OpenAI engineer accused of hiding a bug in a meeting room used by bosses will go on trial on 20 January 2027, Courthouse News reported. US District Judge Vince Chhabria set the date on Tuesday after the defence asked for a speedy trial.

Abdulrahman Alfozan, a 35-year-old Saudi national, has pleaded not guilty to one count of secretly recording private talks, CNBC reported. He faces up to five years in prison if convicted. He has been held without bail since his arrest on 9 October.

Prosecutors say Alfozan, who joined OpenAI in July 2025, plugged the device into a USB port behind a TV in the room. OpenAI staff found it on 30 September. OpenAI has said it found no proof that he took source code or customer data.

At Tuesday’s hearing, the judge said he was worried the defence was not getting all the evidence it should, Courthouse News reported. Prosecutors must hand over FBI reports by 24 November, according to TokenPost.

Prosecutors are also contesting the defence’s plan to use Anthropic’s Claude to review evidence in the case, TokenPost reported.

Alfozan has not been charged with espionage. In October, prosecutors said he had been in frequent WhatsApp contact with a member of the Saudi royal family, CNBC reported.

Former GTA 4 and Red Dead boss thinks "we are one Toy Story moment away" from people accepting gen-AI in game development in the West

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

"In the East, they've already accepted it."

Ben Feder, the former Take-Two CEO who oversaw the launch of massive hits like Grand Theft Auto 4, Red Dead Redemption, Borderlands, and BioShock during his tenure, believes the resistance against gen-AI in games will vanish once the games industry has its 'Toy Story moment'.

"When animation went from hand-drawn to computer animation, there was a massive wall of resistance," Feder told The Game Business. "People didn't think it looked good. Then Pixar launched Toy Story. They created a movie that not only was awesome, but used computer-generated animation and chose a subject matter that made the most of it, and did an amazing job with it."

Feder argued that the success of Toy Story ended the resistance against computer-generated animation, and the same could happen to games. Feder added, "My own view is that we are one Toy Story moment away from consumer acceptance of AI in video games in the West. In the East, they've already accepted it."

Feder, who currently heads up venture capital fund Tirta Ventures, is investing in AI-first games. He, however, doesn't necessarily believe AI will reduce development costs, but that it also offers other advantages that could help game makers keep up with shifting consumer tastes.

"A lot of incumbents think about AI as a way of reducing costs, which it is," Feder said. "I query whether it will actually reduce costs, or will it just make games bigger for the same cost? But we're more interested in what AI can do to radically change gameplay."

Feder views gen-AI simply as new technology that allows developers to do things they couldn't before. He likened it to Unreal Engine's instancing technology, which allowed Fortnite and other battle royale games to support 100+ players in a single server.

"That's question number one as we think about what we invest in: Was this possible before AI?" Feder said of his VC firm's strategy. "And when AI gets better, does the game get better? That's harder for an incumbent to focus on, because they deploy a lot of capital, have enormous advantages in terms of distribution, capital, and IP, and all of that needs to be protected."

There's been enormous pushback from players, developers and pundits against the use of gen-AI in games. Even so, more and more studios have revealed using some gen-AI during development, such as to assist with coding. More broadly, gen-AI adoption is on the rise across the industry, with a recent report estimating 40 percent of developers are using it.

Some development studios, such as Fortnite maker Epic Games, have doubled down on the use of gen-AI in game development. Epic is even more bullish on gen-AI in its Unreal Engine, which the company is currently transforming into an engine that's almost entirely driven by gen-AI and LLMs. Consumer sentiment remains sour, however, and the perception of the tool is not helped by the number of 'AI slop' games taking over Steam's storefront, the 'enshittification' of fan wikis, or the link AI seems to have to the unprecedented wave of layoffs that has shaken the industry over the past two years.

Vitalik Buterin Wants Crypto to Be More Like Buying a House

📅 2026-11-18  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

Ethereum co-founder Vitalik Buterin has said that crypto needs some of the features that made housing a safe investment for earlier generations. "We need the equivalent of those good properties that housing investment has, for modern digital assets," Buterin wrote in a post on X on Nov. 18.

The post came a day after economist Peter Tulip published his own post on housing in Raleigh, North Carolina. According to Tulip, looser zoning rules in the city set off a construction boom and pushed real rents down 15% between 2022 and 2025.

In his post, Buterin first pointed to a benefit of young people putting their savings into stocks and crypto instead of real estate.

"One benefit of younger generations investing more in stonks and other digital things instead of housing is that it reduces perverse incentives that drive people to push to keep housing expensive, instead of rejoicing when prices drop as we do for all other life necessities," he wrote.

Still, the Ethereum co-founder said that housing has had one advantage over stocks and crypto. He described it as "a culture and policy-encouraged default of investing in a safe non-crazy thing that you can't easily liquidate."

"Modern platforms encourage the opposite of this, leading to more irresponsible behavior," Buterin added.

Ether was trading at $1,808 on Wednesday.

Buterin has written about housing and urban planning before. He has supported the YIMBY movement for denser development in cities for several years.

In 2022, Buterin argued for a land value tax. According to him, such a tax would lower the upfront cost of buying a home and discourage speculation on vacant lots.

In 2025, the Ethereum co-founder called the housing market a "confusing market of peculiar NFTs." According to Buterin, what is really being sold under the label of square footage is the right to live in a particular city. At the time, he also said that legalizing dense development speeds up the economy and benefits even people who prefer single-family homes.

I'm learning a lot about Maye Musk—that's Elon Musk's mum—today. She's a model. She was born in Canada. She's hugely popular in China. That last one probably explains why she made an appearance in a marketing video for the new version of Infold Games' dress-up gacha Infinity Nikki, of all things.

‘NAZA’ Team Sticking To Plan To Release Shattering Documentary On The Guardian Website Within Weeks — IDFA

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The explosive documentary NAZA has become a worldwide box office sensation, earning $15 million to date. But if co-directors Yuval Abraham and Rachel Szor had prevailed, it might have gone straight from its Venice Film Festival premiere to The Guardian website.

“At first me and Rachel thought, let’s put it out online the next day,” Abraham explained as he appeared for a panel discussion about the film at International Documentary Festival Amsterdam (IDFA). Their partners on the project convinced the filmmakers that a theatrical release should be part of how NAZA reached viewers.

“It’s different audiences,” commented Fionualla Jamison, managing director of sales agent-distributor mk2. “Online — it’s not necessarily everyone who’s going to be aware, who has that viewing habit to go watch something on The Guardian website or on YouTube, depending what people’s setups are. So, we felt it was something that could absolutely exist alongside for different audiences and at cinemas.” Watch on Deadline

“Our North Star always has been how we’re going to get this film to people,” said Lindsay Poulton, head of documentaries at The Guardian, who produced the film alongside The Guardian’s Harry Davies and James Wilson of JW Films. “I think sitting in a cinema has been an important part of that. We’ve talked publicly as well about how the film is going to be available on our platform.”

Poulton and fellow panelists reiterated The Guardian intends to make the film available for free on its website in early December, although a specific date hasn’t been revealed. In the meantime, the documentary has played theatrically in 50 countries, and recorded a remarkable 1.6 million admissions.

Abraham, part of the Oscar-winning quartet of directors of No Other Land, along with Szor, became a believer in the theatrical component. Appearing on the panel by remote from The Guardian offices in London, he said he realized, “The film being immediately accessible doesn’t necessarily translate to maximum impact. There is something to be gained from also doing this theatrical run.”

NAZA presents interviews with 24 anonymous members of Israeli intelligence and Israel Defense Forces personnel who describe how Israeli military leaders callously disregarded the potential loss of Palestinian civilian life as they pursued Hamas targets in Gaza. The whistleblowers claim this included deliberating striking Hamas militants at night in their homes with full knowledge those attacks would likely lead to the deaths of innocent women and children. The IDF has denied violating human rights standards and the Geneva Convention in its prosecution of the war. It has also denied an assertion in the film that the IDF used AI to select targets.

The release of NAZA has triggered furor within Israel’s right-wing. The country’s Culture Minister, Miki Zohar, vowed to strip Abraham and Szor of their Israeli citizenship, a move later endorsed by Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu. Supporters of the Israeli government went so far as to quickly produce a counter documentary titled NAZA: The Real Story, mimicking the style of Abraham and Szor’s film by shooting it on rooftops at night in Tel Aviv.

“There’s a film out there that’s very misleading. Obviously, it’s not a coincidence,” Abraham stated. “They call themselves NAZA. They’re trying to mislead people into thinking that they are the real NAZA film.”

The director also told the audience at the DeLaMar Theater venue at IDFA that Israel’s military has released “this kind of guidebook for commanders giving them instructions or explaining to them how to speak with their soldiers about NAZA. So, it’s like for commanders in the Israeli Air Force and other officers sort of an explanation as to how you can deal with the claims that are raised in the film. And of course, they say what you’d expect them to say. And very high up it says that the purpose of this guidebook is to strengthen the soldier’s sense of the — they use this term ‘righteousness of the path, that what we are doing is right.’”

Abraham said he didn’t view the existence of the guidebook or the counter documentary in wholly negative terms.

“There’s also something positive about it which I think when you get people to be so defensive, when commanders feel a need to get this kind of guidebook to reassure their soldiers that the path is right and that the army is moral and immune from criticism and that they say NAZA [a Hebrew acronym meaning collateral damage], it’s not a bad term,” he noted. “There’s something also about being defensive, which is a reflection of the sense that you are scrutinized, the sense that you are under magnifying glass… They feel like they’re not only watching, they’re also being watched. They’re not only surveilling in a sense; they’re also being surveilled or looked at. And that’s good.”

James Wilson, the Oscar-nominated producer (Zone of Interest) said the message of NAZA won’t be derailed by efforts at misinformation.

“It’s about horror. It’s about mass murder that our society, the West, has been completely implicit in,” he insisted. “It couldn’t be about something more important and serious and sort of abyssal in its horror… It’s like an alarm. It’s a fucking alarm and we all have to bear witness to that as it’s happening.”

Abraham concluded by saying, “People talk about films changing the world. I don’t think that a film can really do that, but I think films can definitely, as Jim [Wilson] was saying, they can definitely be part of a network, of a larger kind of energy that exists in the world, which is pushing for change, which is pushing for humanity and empathy.”

GTA 6 Custom PS5 Pro Gifted To Miami Heat Players

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sam Woods is TheGamer’s Senior Reporter. He’s been in games journalism since 2020 and has undertaken a variety of roles in that time. These have included roles such as SEO Editor, Managing Editor at DualShockers, and Global Head of Gaming, and he's also been seen on the BBC. He was promoted to the Senior Reporter position in 2025 following a series of high-quality reports and exclusives for TheGamer.

Since 2020, Sam has written over 3,000 articles and specializes in telling the stories of the people behind the games. He’s interviewed the biggest game directors, some of the industry’s top talents, told the stories of how indie games came to be, and spoken to players about what really makes them tick. He’s also interviewed some of the world’s biggest musical artists to find out how the medium has inspired their work, and TV and Film actors about their roles in video game adaptations. He even hosted a PAX panel in 2021 and made a documentary in 2024 surrounding the creation of a Disney game.

Alongside this, Sam focuses on breaking news. He got the scoop on Perfect Dark’s cancellation, provided context on some AI drama, and revealed that Lady Dimitrescu’s actress would love to step back into the character’s giant shoes. These articles have been picked up by the biggest gaming sites, including IGN, GameSpot, and PC Gamer. He also loves to dive into the numbers behind the games.

Sam’s journey in games journalism has taken him all over the world. He’s covered Gamescom in Germany, Summer Game Fest in Los Angeles, and Tokyo Game Show in Japan, each on three occasions.

He’s been learning Japanese since 2024 with the intention of identifying and translating Japanese-language news and bringing it to English-language audiences first, something he’s done countless times.

Sam’s first review was 2020’s Battletoads reboot, and he’s since reviewed games in The Legend of Zelda, Super Mario, and Pokemon franchises.

In recent years, there have been some stunning (and not-so-stunning) limited-edition custom consoles. These are often created to promote a game and are usually available in limited quantities via competitions and giveaways. In 2019, Xbox commissioned 12 custom Xbox One consoles to celebrate the launch of Sekiro: Shadows Die Twice, and more recently, it shoved a Series X down an alligator's throat.

This new one is a real head-turner, though, and it's one that you'll almost certainly never see in person, as Rockstar just gifted players of Miami Heat custom Grand Theft Auto 6 PlayStation 5 Pro consoles.

Grand Theft Auto's Vice City is heavily based on Miami. Grand Theft Auto 6 takes that a step further, with Leonida looking very much like Rockstar's version of Florida. The parallels are so great, in fact, that Rockstar turned the city of Miami into Vice City.

It's painted electrical boxes, taken over the airport, and a nightclub has temporarily changed its name to match the one in GTA 6. It's also opened up Burger Shot shops for people to get GTA's burgers in real life, and perhaps most notably, will take over the Miami Heat basketball team for one night. It's set to wear limited-edition jerseys, and the court has been redecorated with the infamous blue and pink of Vice City.

The collaboration seemingly didn't stop there, either. Over the last few hours, Miami Heat players, including Myron Gardner, Davion Mitchell, and Tim Hardaway Jr., have posted stunning PS5 Pro care packages they've received from Rockstar.

Delivered in a custom suitcase, the players received two limited-edition Grand Theft Auto 6 PS5 DualSense controllers, a copy of Grand Theft Auto 6, and a custom PS5 Pro console. The console is a slick silver color, etched with palm trees and the VI from the GTA logo. The vent running across the middle is metallic pink and blue.

These are some of the coolest consoles we've ever seen, and for what will likely be the biggest media event for many, many years. It's just a shame that we'll probably never get to lay our eyes on them in person.

FAA suspends installation of router that led to a ground stop of flights across the US

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The router malfunctioned and flooded other routers with data, prompting the FAA to order the ground stop.

The Federal Aviation Administration (FAA) has suspended the installation of a router model made by Hewlett-Packard Enterprise-owned Juniper Networks, which was part of its modernization efforts. As The New York Times reports, the agency believes the router played a major role in the issue that caused it to order a ground stop for all flights in the US on November 13. In addition to suspending new installations, it has also disabled more than 60 routers that had already been installed.

If you'll recall, Transport Secretary Sean Duffy blamed the ground stop on old hardware the first time he spoke about the event. But Duffy revealed a day later that the issue was connected to the modernization of America's aviation facilities.

He said a contractor from tech company L3Harris was working on switching telecommunication lines from old copper wires to fiber and on installing the new router at an FAA site in El Dorado, Arkansas. That router started flooding other routers in the air traffic control system with data. Another router in Amarillo, Texas had also gone haywire, and the FAA responded by disabling both of them.

The agency said the router was tested for a year before installations started. "Sixty-eight times it worked well," he sad at a news conference. "This one router started to broadcast into our network, and it started to overload the network." In aviation, routers are used to transmit data, including radar feeds, flight plans and weather information.

Despite knowing that it was the router that caused the issue, FAA administrator Bryan Bedford said they still don't know what triggered it. The Senate Commerce, Science and Transportation Committee's Democratic members are urging the agency to find out why it had malfunctioned. They also asked the FAA to report to Congress on how it's going to prevent the same thing from happening in the future.

Duffy said he was told by national security agencies that the issue wasn't caused by a cyberattack, but he still asked the FBI to be part of the team analyzing the router from Arkansas. The Transport Secretary has also renewed his calls for Congress to approve an additional $50 billion in funding to modernize America's air traffic control system. "I would just tell you, the longer we stay on an old rickety network, the less safe it becomes," he said. "We do have to get this work done. Otherwise, staying on the old system for longer is going to mean more risk as opposed to fixing the system, and you're going to reduce the risk."

The Brazilian forward struggled to find his best form at the Emirates Stadium, and he feels he was simply in the wrong place at the wrong time

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Timing is everything in football - and that goes for getting transfer moves right, too.

After seven years at Chelsea, Willian made the move across London to join Arsenal in 2020, which seemed like it should be a decent enough fit for all concerned.

But Willian has made no secret of the fact that he did not enjoy his time at the Emirates Stadium - and in hindsight, he believes he may simply have been the wrong player in the wrong place at the wrong time.

Willian told FourFourTwo: "These things happen in football. I had some personal frustrations and I couldn't perform on the pitch the way I had been performing at Chelsea.

"Arsenal were also a club that was changing a lot at that time. There were a lot of people leaving and a lot of new people coming in.

"It was a restructuring and I got caught up in the middle of it - and it's not easy.

I left a well-established club, winning almost every year, fighting for titles every year, and I joined a club that was trying to find a way to win titles again.

The best features, fun and footballing quizzes, straight to your inbox every week.

"So I got caught up in that situation, and that's why things didn't work out. It wasn't easy, but I really wasn't happy.

"The contract I had was actually a very big one, but it got to the point that money wasn’t enough.

"The most important thing is to be happy, to play with joy. I wasn't able to do so there, so I ended up asking to leave the club, leaving that contract behind."

After spending a year back in Brazil with Corinthians, Willian found a new home at Fulham in 2022 - and he credits Marco Silva with helping him to rediscover his spark.

He said: "When I left Brazil, my goal was to go to England, stay there, because I love the place and my agent lives there too.

"Therefore, I decided to be in London, have some meetings and see what the possibilities are. My agent said, ‘Marco Silva wants to talk to you’. We had a meeting with him, and he invited me to join training and see how I felt.

"It wasn't a trial, it was more to see if I could readjust, especially after what had happened to me at Arsenal.

"I really liked Marco’s style. I felt celebrated, and it's not flattery, but I felt that respect, that affection from everyone. I was very happy there at Fulham."

Steven still covers Huddersfield Town home and away for his own publication, WeAreTerriers.com. Steven is a two-time nominee for Regional Journalist of the Year at the prestigious British Sports Journalism Awards, making the shortlist in 2020 and 2023.

President Donald Trump has landed in Shenzhen for historic trilateral talks with Chinese leader Xi Jinping and Russia's Vladimir Putin.

Trump is in China for the APEC summit, which President Xi opened with calls for free trade and greater cooperation around artificial intelligence.

He said: "We should actively build partnerships for scientific and technological innovation, deepen cooperation in the digital economy and artificial intelligence and promote a green and low-carbon transition."

Putin has also addressed the summit, claiming sanctions imposed on Russia have backfired and strengthened his nation.

The much-anticipated meeting between the three world leaders is set to take place on Thursday, with discussions expected to center on the Iran war and Russia's war in Ukraine.

Though expectations for any concrete outcome are low, Trump, Xi and Putin are expected to hold in-depth discussions on issues crucial for world peace and strategic stability, a spokesperson for China's foreign ministry said Wednesday.

North Korean leader Kim Jong-Un could potentially attend the summit, which is a 21-member gathering of Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation Leaders.

Trump flew in from Manila, Philippines, where he started a three-day visit to Asia and met key regional allies including Japan, India and Australia.

The APEC host city Shenzhen has been dubbed one of China's "Silicon Valleys" and has been a major hub for China's internet and artificial intelligence companies.

The summit offers an opportunity for Beijing to present itself as a steward of multilateralism against what it terms "unilateralism and protectionism," with trade, artificial intelligence, and the Middle East conflict expected to dominate the meeting's agenda.

Xi held several bilateral meetings with attending leaders on the sidelines Wednesday, including South Korean President Lee Jae Myung, to discuss trade, commerce and peace on the Korean Peninsula. Earlier this week, Xi also met Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney and Vietnamese President To Lam.

Xi also had met with Putin earlier Wednesday, before Trump's arrival.

OpenAI to Surface Le Figaro Reporting in ChatGPT Partnership

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

OpenAI and Groupe Figaro announced a strategic partnership on November 18, 2026, to make current and archived Le Figaro journalism discoverable in ChatGPT through summaries and excerpts that link to original stories. Le Figaro also plans to deploy ChatGPT Enterprise across various teams.

The agreement covers Le Figaro and associated publications, spanning news, business, culture and lifestyle. Including archive material puts more than the latest headlines into the arrangement, while links to underlying stories give readers a route from ChatGPT’s condensed presentation to the publisher’s full reporting. That makes the original article part of the intended reading path alongside the summary or excerpt.

Groupe Figaro CEO Marc Feuillée said the partnership would combine OpenAI’s technological expertise and global reach with the group’s editorial heritage and trusted brands, supported by more than 600 journalists, to create innovation opportunities and long-term value for readers.

The combination explains the business logic of the arrangement. Groupe Figaro brings current and archival coverage backed by that editorial operation, while OpenAI provides a new interface for discovering the work and the technology used to present it within ChatGPT.

Varun Shetty, OpenAI’s vice president of media partnerships, described the agreement as part of OpenAI’s commitment to supporting quality journalism and reaching new audiences. “Le Figaro’s reporting helps readers make sense of the stories shaping France and the wider world, and we want AI to help more people discover that work,” he said.

Le Figaro will retain full editorial independence and control over its journalism. That provision keeps newsroom decisions with the publisher as its work becomes discoverable through an AI interface. OpenAI supplies the ChatGPT channel and presentation format, while Le Figaro remains responsible for the reporting being surfaced.

For OpenAI, the partnership builds on work with news organizations around the world focused on three areas: improving discovery of reliable information, developing responsible AI applications for publishers, and creating new ways for audiences to engage with original reporting.

The internal technology plans are distinct from the content-discovery agreement. Le Figaro intends to introduce ChatGPT Enterprise across various teams to support day-to-day work, extending the relationship from audience-facing distribution into the publisher’s own operations.

It will also explore additional uses of OpenAI technology in workflows and reader services. Those explorations will be guided by Le Figaro’s AI charter and editorial standards, making those internal rules the stated framework for how the publisher expands its use of AI.

The arrangement gives Le Figaro a new distribution channel without transferring editorial authority. The enterprise and reader-service plans add a second dimension: the publisher’s AI charter and editorial standards will guide how OpenAI technology enters its own operations, making internal governance as important to the partnership as discovery in ChatGPT.

Gemini 4 Hydrogen: a faster model for general purpose robotics

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Over the past month, Gemini 4 Argon and Gemini 4 Carbon brought frontier physical reasoning to robotics: understanding 3D scenes, reconstructing real environments in simulation, practicing tasks in sim before running them on hardware, and directly controlling robot arms from camera images. Last week, we introduced Gemini 4 Hydrogen, which brings strong spatial and embodied reasoning to a much faster model built for speed and efficiency.

To help developers put Hydrogen to work, today we're launching the Robotics plugin on Google Antigravity: a set of skills for hardware onboarding, simulation and robot control turning Antigravity into a one-stop robotics workbench. Together, they enable a developer to go from a robot in a box to one that can perform a working application in an afternoon. To install the plugin, open Antigravity, go to Customizations and search for Robotics on the Build With Google shelf.

Gemini 4 Argon and Carbon showed that a general model can drive a robot arm directly, and Gemini 4 Hydrogen preserves that ability. Give it a goal in plain language and a camera view, and it plans the task, writes and runs the control code, and keeps adjusting until the job is done. On the Artificial Analysis Robotics 0.1 benchmark, Gemini 4 Hydrogen controls a real 6-axis arm through eight everyday tabletop tasks, from pouring a drink to packing blocks into a box. Hydrogen scores 58, similar to Argon at a lower cost per token.

Hydrogen brings the specialized skills of our Gemini Robotics ER 2 (Embodied Reasoning) models into the core Gemini 4 model series, and it beats them on nearly every axis. On the public ERQA benchmark, Hydrogen outperforms Gemini Robotics ER 2 on spatial reasoning tasks such as multi-view correspondence and 3D trajectory reasoning. For developers using Gemini Robotics ER 2 today, we recommend trying out Gemini 4 Hydrogen for these workloads.

A fast, capable model is only half the story. Building a robot application has always meant gluing together drivers, calibration scripts, simulators, perception code, and a UI, which requires robotics expertise and a lot of time. The new Robotics plugin for Google Antigravity packages that work into skills Gemini can use on your behalf:

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Here's what that looks like end to end: set up a robot and build real applications.

Starting from an empty desk, a developer plugs an I2RT YAM arm and an external depth camera into a laptop and asks Hydrogen in Antigravity to bring it up. The plugin's Skills install the software, find the camera, calibrate the arm, and run test motions. The arm is calibrated and ready for tasks in about 10 minutes. A similar flow also brings up a low-cost SO-ARM101 arm and an industrial grade FR3 Duo from scratch to live waving.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Once the robot is live, you can use a simple prompt to create a basic task within just a few minutes:

Once the robot is live, the next step is building a simulation you can trust. With the Robotics plugin, MuJoCo runs directly inside Antigravity with dedicated creation, auditing, and rendering skills. This allows you to build and preview high-quality simulated scenes and assets—whether described, imported from links/documents, or uploaded via workspace photos—right alongside your code. You can then "vibe sim" by adjusting shapes, positions, and physical properties through chat to verify reaching and grasping before deploying to physical hardware.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

The workbench with Hydrogen and Robotics plugins lets you go from an idea to a repeatable working application in real world in a much shorter time, by iterating and validating it in simulation.

A developer starts with a whiteboard sketch: a conveyor belt carrying mixed snacks, with a robot arm picking and sorting them into bins. From one prompt of the task with images of a real scene, Hydrogen constructs an interactive MuJoCo simulation of the cell, testing and validating the grasping policy in sim across multiple runs. The developer then deploys the verified policy directly to the robot in the real world. When nuances emerge (such as tuning gripper closure force or refining sorting rules), the developer iterates in natural language, asking Hydrogen to adjust the controller to iterate. Within about an hour end-to-end, the arm reliably sorts snacks off the moving belt.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Hydrogen's efficiency makes it practical to run a multi-step robot application all day. In this example, a developer uses Hydrogen to build a small web storefront for a soap shop, then connects it to two YAM Pro arms. When an order comes in, the arms set out a packing bin, pick the ordered mint and citrus bars, and pull a printed thank-you note from a printer.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

With just a few prompts, the FR3 Duo simulates a chess board and then can play chess against itself or take on human opponents.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Gemini 4 Argon pushes the ceiling of physical reasoning. Gemini 4 Hydrogen makes those capabilities fast and affordable enough to use every day, and the Antigravity Robotics plugin puts them in a workbench where anyone can go from a robot in a box to a working application. We can't wait to see what you build.

You can share your own creations and use cases on the plugin’s GitHub discussions page. If you have any ideas, custom skills, or derived work you think would be helpful, please let us know—we’ll keep improving the plugins.

Accelerating development for massive Git repositories

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Android’s source code is big – the Android 17 open source release has 1,084 Git repositories, 1.62 million files, and takes 300 GB for a built workspace. Additionally, in a typical Android source tree with hardware vendor code and first-party applications, the size increases by 2-3x. Traditionally, it took over 30 minutes to fetch Android Open Source Project (AOSP) source code and well over an hour to build on even highly capable developer workstations. As software development evolved over the last 2 years, modern coding harnesses and LLMs have accelerated the development of many projects. These tools are used today for Android operating system development, but the long sync and build times make them less efficient. Both human developers and agents need quick setup, parallel workspaces, and fast build-and-test iteration times.

We built Another Build File Service (ABFS) to address these bottlenecks. ABFS is a full suite of software across a Linux kernel filesystem module, user-space daemons, and server-side infrastructure. ABFS uses content-addressability across the entire stack to lazy-load and cache source code, developer workspaces, intermediate build files, and build results.

Today, we are excited to announce that ABFS is open source on GitHub.The impact of ABFS to Android is:

ABFS already powers parallel workflows for developers and agents in Android, but it is designed to scale to other massive Git-based projects.

In the Android build system, developers define build rules in Blueprint files (Android.bp), Soong compiles them into Ninja files, and Ninja executes the final build graph (see the Android build overview). Similar to the Make build tool, Ninja is timestamp-based. If a build step’s input file is newer than the existing output file, Ninja rebuilds the output file.

Analyzing the AOSP code for Android 17 shows the sheer disk footprint and file count involved in a single checkout and build.

In a typical Android tree that includes hardware-specific repositories, board support packages, first-party applications, form-factor-specific code, etc., the code size is 2–3 times the size of AOSP (over 3,000 Git repositories), compounded across multiple active SKU and legacy maintenance branches.

Building the Cuttlefish emulator target executes a build graph with 192,249 Ninja actions. While local incremental builds are fast once a tree is already built, every new workspace, branch switch, or ephemeral agent session starts cold.Even though typical developer changes touch only 1-10 different Git projects, someone building locally without any remote execution might waste up to 80 minutes on a 64-core machine repeating work their CI system already built. Even if a developer edits a core library like system/libbase, 97% of the build graph is identical to the CI build.

Sharing cached build outputs across CI and local machines is also tricky in a codebase of Android's age and rate of change. Build rules occasionally introduce hidden dependencies (such as passing an undeclared schema file inside raw compiler flags), over-declared inputs, or undeclared outputs. In timestamp-based builds, hidden dependencies silently produce stale incremental artifacts, forcing developers to wipe output directories and run clean builds.

ABFS makes building Android fast by replacing massive local source checkouts with a virtual, content-addressable filesystem (CASFS). ABFS streams files on demand only when a build actually touches them and utilizes shared build caching to help eliminate redundant downloads and compilation.

On the server side, Git-pushers continuously ingest new Git objects from Gerrit or other Git hosts into a horizontally scalable gRPC ABFS Content-Addressable Storage (CAS) Server that indexes Git content, workspace snapshots, and build step results. On the developer host, a local Least Recently Used (LRU) Cacheman daemon, Mount Daemon, and Tap utility connect user-space build tools to the kernel filesystem so checkouts and builds stream only what they need. See the full ABFS architecture on GitHub.

At the core of the client is a virtual filesystem called Content-Addressable Storage Filesystem (CASFS), a Linux kernel module that exposes Git SHA content hashes via extended attributes. When a file's hash is set, CASFS populates its metadata immediately and downloads file bytes via Cacheman only when needed:

Initial checkouts download only lightweight directory metadata prepopulated with Git hashes. CASFS uses Git's object model natively, allowing standard git and repo operations to work transparently.

CASFS can also tap build processes. Tapping records every file actually read, written, or deleted into lists called patches, which are stored alongside cached build outputs. Ninja-go replaces timestamp checks with two-phase content-addressable caching:

Ninja-go accelerates Ninja-based builds like Android and Chrome; other build systems can integrate with ABFS's caching APIs using the same model (see the two-phase caching walkthrough on GitHub).

Each mounted checkout attaches to a persistent workspace that eagerly syncs modified files, git state, and repo state through Cacheman to the ABFS CAS Server—keeping mounts synchronized across sessions or across multiple developer hosts.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

The benchmarks below show the mean time of 3 Android runs on a GCE n2d-standard-64 VM with 64 vCPUs and 256 GB of RAM. The ABFS remote server was running in the same cloud network as the client.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

Because ABFS does lazy loading and local cache deduplication, developers and their agents can now mount multiple concurrent Android workspaces on a single machine, benefit from a shared local build cache, and achieve massive scale in development time.

An agent can check out a full Android tree in under 10 seconds and perform a full build in under 10 minutes. Agent swarms are empowered to iterate, review, and test in parallel, which can lead to higher quality solutions and speed.In the demo below, an agent makes a system/libbase fix on the latest AOSP release, then 4 parallel agents simultaneously backport, build, and test the fix across 4 more AOSP releases on a single VM.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Link to Youtube Video (visible only when JS is disabled)

We released ABFS as open source because we want developers, Android partners, and any large-scale Git-based project to benefit from what we have learned and be able to scale.

While Android’s Soong/Ninja pipeline was the first acceleration target, ABFS was built to be extensible to other build systems. Content-addressable build systems like Bazel map naturally onto CASFS and there is already a proof-of-concept integration with Bazel. Chromium, the Android Common Kernel, and other Google projects are exploring ABFS compatibility.

Explore ABFS, CASFS, and Ninja-go on GitHub, and reach out to us at abfs@google.com.

For help with the Google Cloud server-side, infrastructure-as-code, and CASFS for Cloud Workstations and Kubernetes, please reach out to abfs-gcp@google.com.

8 Biggest Snubs in the 2026 Game Awards Nominations

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The Game Awards has announced its official list of nominees for this year’s prizes. Among the nominees are plenty of obvious contenders, such as Grand Theft Auto 6, Resident Evil Requiem, and The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time. There was stiff competition across several categories, meaning that some worthy games — and Polygon favorites — didn’t make the shortlist for certain prizes. These are the biggest TGA nomination snubs of 2026.

As one eagle-eyed Resetera user pointed out, Sony just broke a streak that dates all the way back to 2015. This marks the first time in 11 years that a first-party PlayStation release hasn't received a Game of the Year nomination. During that 11-year streak, Sony took home three GOTY trophies: God of War (2018), The Last of Us Part 2 (2020), and Astro Bot (2024). This year's big first-party game from PlayStation was Marvel's Wolverine, which is currently sitting at a 75 on Metacritic and incited a whole lot of debate about difficulty and UI design. The game was nominated in two categories: Best Performance for Liam McIntyre and Innovation in Accessibility.

There were a ton of fantastic video game performances this year, and somebody’s favorite was bound to get left out. Fire Emblem: Fortune’s Weave fan favorite Jimmie Yamaguchi gave an outstanding performance as the oddball scholar Fabio, and by every measure, it deserved a nomination. In less capable hands, Fabio’s starry-eyed devotion to Fortune’s Weave co-protagonist Dietrich could have been played for laughs, but Yamaguchi makes the budding romance between these men believable and heartfelt.

Manni L. Perez was nominated for GTA 6, presumably for portraying Lucia Caminos. (Funnily enough, developer Rockstar Games has not yet officially confirmed Perez is actually playing Lucia, despite longstanding online rumors.) Apparently, the actor who played co-lead Jason Duval didn’t make enough of an impact with players to score a nomination.

Blizzard’s World of Warcraft has had a massive year, with the launch of its reimagined Classic experience, World of Warcraft: Forever. The team also released major updates for the modern version of the beloved MMORPG, like March’s Midnight expansion. Those changes weren’t enough to move the needle with voters, and WoW was also left out of the Best Community Support category.

The popular farming sim, which entered early access in 2024 before its full release this year, may have more than 26,000 “overwhelmingly positive” reviews on Steam, but that wasn’t enough for it to snag a nomination in any category. Best Debut Indie might have been Fields of Mistria’s best shot at a prize, but it wouldn’t have been out of place in Best Sim/Strategy or the broader Best Indie category, either.

Despite a wobbly third act, Fortune’s Weave is one of our favorite games of 2026, one of the best games available for the Nintendo Switch 2, and one of the best-reviewed games of the year. Surprisingly, developer Intelligent Systems’ epic strategy RPG only managed to pick up one nomination, for Best Role-Playing Game. It stands a decent chance of taking this one home: other nominees include Final Fantasy Resonance, Monster Hunter Stories 3, The Blood of Dawnwalker, and Trails in the Sky 2nd Chapter.

Pearl Abyss’s open-world adventure game was an early-year frontrunner for a GOTY nom, even after middling reviews and a rocky launch. While it wasn’t a critical darling, it did have a pretty massive online following that made the prospect of several nominations seem likely. Surprisingly, Crimson Desert will compete in just one category: Best Community Support.

Bungie’s extraction shooter managed to snag a nomination for Best Multiplayer Game, which it frankly stands little chance of winning against a field that includes two indie chart-toppers and a well-regarded Call of Duty. However, the stylish game was overlooked in the Best Art Direction category, perhaps partly due to the shadow cast by a 2025 plagiarism scandal where Bungie admitted to using an artist’s designs without proper credit. (Bungie later credited her as a Visual Design Consultant.) Marathon’s retro-future meets Y2K cyberpunk aesthetic truly stands out in a sea of Unreal Engine 5 homogeneity, and it’s a shame to see it overlooked here.

Both the Best Indie and Best Debut Indie categories have some stiff competition this year. However, no independent game managed to get a nomination for Game of the Year in 2026. While there weren’t a ton of indie contenders for the top category this year, Polygon’s Oli Welsh had the co-op adventure Big Walk pegged as a dark horse, though developer House House’s recent AI controversy likely didn’t help matters.

The lack of an indie GOTY nominee represents a pretty massive shift from the 2025 crop, which included a record-setting three games developed by independent studios: Hades 2, Hollow Knight: Silksong, and Clair Obscur: Expedition 33. Sandfall Interactive’s much-loved RPG managed to take home the top prize and a total of nine Game Awards, the most wins by a single game. 2024 also saw one indie crack the category, Balatro, though the top honors went to Astro Bot.

The Game Awards 2026 will stream live on Disney+, YouTube, and Twitch, on Thursday, Dec. 10, starting at 7:30 p.m. EST / 4:30 p.m. PST.

Will Grand Theft Auto 6 take home Game of the Year?

Google expands access to Gemini Tasks, which can help with calls, purchases

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Google is expanding the rollout of Tasks, a toggle in Gemini that converts the chatbot into a more active helper.

Unlike Gemini’s Chat, which merely allows users to talk to Google’s AI assistant for informational purposes, Tasks empowers the app to complete basic secretarial functions, deputizing it as a kind of personal assistant.

How, specifically, can you use it? Google says that the app can call businesses and book appointments on users’ behalf; it can also complete purchases for them via Google Pay, and even sign into websites securely if prompted to do so.

In a blog post, Josh Woodword, VP of Gemini, claims to have recently used the feature to call local car dealerships to schedule an appointment for his family’s minivan. He says he also used it to analyze his electrical bill.

Getting the function up and running is easy. Users merely flip a toggle within the app from Chat to Tasks, which enables the feature. Users can also send texts to Gemini or call the app to give it commands.

Seattle tech vet and Sounders FC minority owner Chee Chew joins front office to lead AI push

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

by Kurt Schlosser on Nov 17, 2026 at 11:02 amNovember 17, 2026 at 11:02 am

Chee Chew helped build the Windows 95 Start menu, started Google Hangouts and ran consumer engagement at Amazon. Now the Seattle tech veteran is taking on a new job with the Sounders and Reign FC, where he’ll lead how the soccer clubs use technology, data and AI.

Chew, a minority owner of Sounders FC since 2019, is joining the organization’s executive team as chief technology and transformation officer, reporting to majority owner Adrian Hanauer, the club announced Tuesday.

“His knowledge of and passion for the Sounders and Reign allow him to hit the ground running,” Hanauer said in a statement. “His experience leading teams through major shifts in technology gives us a unique opportunity to strengthen both the soccer and business sides of our organization.”

Chew most recently was chief product and technology officer at Zapier, where he helped steer the workflow automation company toward AI. Before that he was chief product officer at mParticle and at Twilio, where he oversaw the communications company’s $3.2 billion acquisition of Segment.

He spent four years at Amazon, nearly eight years as a vice president of engineering at Google, where he also led the company’s Seattle and Kirkland offices, and 14 years at Microsoft working on Internet Explorer, Windows Mobile and Xbox Live.

Chew said his direct team will stay small, but its work will reach every corner of the business, including HR, ticket sales, customer service, match-day planning, corporate partnerships, legal, and player recruiting and scouting. He also wants every department to have its own “builders,” staffers who can create AI tools and automations for their own teams.

“In order to do a change like this, it’s more than technology,” Chew told GeekWire. “Technology is in many ways the easiest part. Driving change, getting deep, building side by side with folks, I think is what it takes.”

Chew didn’t grow up playing soccer. “My family was a very traditional Chinese family of study and play piano,” he said. “It was a stereotype, but it was true for my family.” He caught the bug in college, where he played a ton of intramurals, and later from his wife, Christine, who played through college, and their two kids.

The couple joined the Sounders ownership group in 2019 as one of 11 Seattle-rooted families, many with deep ties to the region’s tech industry. Former Microsoft executive Terry Myerson pulled the group together, and it included Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella, CFO Amy Hood, and others.

A group of owners has met every other Friday since 2019, and “leveraging technology has always been part of the strategy” for the club, Chew said.

The job grew out of a demo Chew built for Hanauer about two and a half years ago: an AI agent that could handle complicated fan requests, such as a season ticket holder with four seats who wants to buy a block of eight. The club later brought in consultants to train staff on AI, but the effort never quite gained momentum, he said.

Talks about a bigger role started early this year and came together by midyear. “Adrian and I talked about it, and we agreed for me to come on board and take an operational role and hack on some code,” Chew said.

“I think I’ve created more code in the past six months than I did in the past 20 years. It’s pretty amazing. I’m having a ton of fun.”

Chew describes Hanauer as a visionary on “the art of the possible” who comes in daily asking, “Could you do this?” Recently, as Chew was boarding a flight home to Seattle, Hanauer called from a meeting where staff doubted they could scale up one effort tenfold without hiring more people. Could AI help?

On the flight, while using the plane’s Wi-Fi, he built a prototype with Anthropic’s Claude. “By the time I landed, I sent it to them and said, ‘OK, give this a try,’” he said. The club used it in an experiment to scale up performance marketing before a match.

It’s the kind of hands-on building Chew says he’s been doing a lot of lately.

“I think I’ve created more code in the past six months than I did in the past 20 years,” he said. “It’s pretty amazing. I’m having a ton of fun.”

He recently ran an AI analysis of one player’s game, based on stats such as touches, expected goals and distance run. It pointed out strengths and areas to improve. The player was “very hungry for it,” Chew said, and asked if they could do it every game.

The club’s performance analytics group, led by data analyst Tyler Cox, was already ahead of much of the organization, and Chew said he’s helping speed up that work with AI.

The bigger challenge is getting people to change how they work, Chew said. At Zapier, he saw many companies struggle to get results from AI, with some spending “massive token budgets” without a big win. He said the answer is blending AI with traditional automation and fitting it into how people already work.

“It has to be approachable for folks,” he said. “You can’t go in and just throw everything up and start from scratch.”

The Sounders host Real Salt Lake in the wild-card round of the MLS Cup Playoffs on Thursday at Lumen Field. Asked whether AI could help figure out a win, Chew passed.

“I don’t want to get involved and interfere with our coaching staff,” he said.
