Vistra Is Down 41% From Its High. History Suggests This Is What Happens Next.

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The leading power producer faces near-term headwinds.

Vistra(VST -1.77%), the largest competitive power producer in America, reached a record high of $217.02 per share on Sept. 22, 2025, marking a 556% gain over its previous two years. The rapid expansion of the power-hungry data center market fueled most of that growth.

But today, Vistra's stock trades at just $128 per share. Let's see what caused that 41% drop, and whether it's still a good long-term play in the AI infrastructure and electrification markets.

Vistra operates natural gas, nuclear, coal, solar, and battery energy storage facilities with a combined capacity of 49 GW. Its retail subsidiaries -- including TXU Energy, Dynegy, Homefield Energy, Ambit, and other regional power companies -- serve over five million customers. It's also exposed to the data center boom through its power purchase agreements (PPAs) with Meta Platforms (META -0.77%) and Amazon(AMZN -0.49%).

Here's how rapidly its revenue and adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA) from ongoing operations grew over the past year.

Data source: Vistra. YOY = Year-over-year

Vistra's growth looks lumpy, but several temporary issues distorted it. Its revenue fell in the third quarter of 2025, due to a tough year-over-year comparison against the record-breaking summer heat waves in Texas in 2024. Its decline in the second quarter of 2026 was driven by unrealized losses on its open derivative positions to lock in future power prices.

Vistra shut down a major portfolio of its Moss Landing battery storage facilities after they were damaged by fires in early 2025, rather than reopening them. PJM Interconnection also extended its existing cap on electricity capacity prices across the Mid-Atlantic and Midwest.

But despite all of that pressure, Vistra continued to grow as the cloud infrastructure and AI markets expanded. That acceleration turned the slow-growth utility into a high-growth AI play.

For the full year, analysts expect Vistra's revenue and adjusted EBITDA to rise 24% and 25%, respectively. With an enterprise value of $64 billion, it looks like a screaming bargain at nine times next year's adjusted EBITDA.

However, Vistra trades at that discount valuation because it faces some unpredictable near-term challenges in Texas, which accounts for over 40% of its total capacity. Texas' governor-elect Gina Hinojosa, who will take office on Jan. 19, plans to freeze residential electricity prices and issue a new moratorium on all new data center approvals until tighter laws are passed. Several other Tier-1 data center states -- including Georgia and Ohio -- are adopting similar strategies.

That pressure, along with the threat of rising interest rates curbing AI spending, could throttle Vistra's growth over the next few years. That's why analysts expect its revenue and adjusted EBITDA to only grow at CAGRs of 6% and 8%, respectively, from 2026 to 2028.

Those growth rates remain stable, but they make Vistra look more like a traditional utility than a higher-growth AI infrastructure play. That's why its stock pulled back sharply from its all-time high, and why its upside could be limited for at least the next 12 months.

Vistra's downside could also be limited at these levels, but I don't think it's a compelling investment yet. Its forward dividend yield of 0.7% is paltry compared with other utility stocks, and it lacks the momentum of higher-growth AI infrastructure stocks. I wouldn't touch it unless the regulatory headwinds dissipate and the data center market heats up again.

IEEE Celebrates 10 Years of Incorporating Ethics Into AI Systems

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Standards and certifications foster socially responsible products

Kathy Pretz is the editor in chief of The Institute, IEEE's member publication

Policymakers, technologists, and members of the public have raised concerns about the harm AI’s rapid advancement might cause to society. Their concerns include bias, data privacy violations, the spread of misinformation, and losing control of AI agents.

Those issues are relatively new to many people, but the IEEE Standards Association has been working for the past decade on ways to prioritize the responsible development of AI systems.

In 2016 IEEE SA launched the IEEE Global Initiative for Ethical Considerations in Autonomous and Intelligent Systems (A/IS) and published the Ethically Aligned Design report. EAD’s mission is to encourage everyone involved in designing autonomous and intelligent systems to prioritize human well-being by applying ethical considerations for the benefit of humanity.

Developed by a multidisciplinary community of experts from around the world, the report outlined eight foundational principles for A/IS. They include human rights, people’s well-being, and transparency. More than 100 leading AI ethics experts provided input. EAD’s Version II report was released in 2017. Both versions solicited public input and received hundreds of pages of feedback, according to John C. Havens, who was the global initiative’s founding executive director, and is now the global director for IEEE Planet Positive 2030. And by 2019, there were more than 400 contributors and the community continues to grow.

“Ethically Aligned Design was the first of its kind in the sense of a report that was more than a list of principles and from an organization with the credibility of IEEE,” Havens says. “At the time it was released, there wasn’t anything like it.”

Since its launch, a series of standards has been developed to provide practical guidelines on designing, building, and deploying technology that prioritizes human values. Several AI ethics certification programs have been introduced.

This year marks the 10th anniversary of EAD’s first draft. To celebrate, IEEE SA recently launched a website, a podcast series, and a blog that reflect on the report’s achievements and impact.

The word ethics is often used interchangeably with morality, but that is not the case in responsible AI systems design, according to EAD’s first version. The report includes insights from classical ethics traditions and value-based design methodologies to offer actionable recommendations for engineers developing AI tools.

The report established eight foundational general principles for designing autonomous systems: respecting human rights, prioritizing increased well-being as success criteria for development, empowering individuals with data agency to have control over their identity, effectiveness, transparency, accountability, awareness of misuse, and competence for the system creators.

The principles shift applied ethics to the design stage of AI systems, compared with being a concern after they’re released, the authors wrote.

The IEEE 7000 series of standards has been developed based on EAD’s framework. Currently the series consists of 29 projects, 11 of which have been published, and more are still being added. They cover topics such as ethical system design model process (IEEE 7000-2021), AI system transparency (IEEE 7001-2021), data privacy (IEEE 7002-2022), algorithmic bias considerations (IEEE 7003-2024), the impact on people’s well-being (IEEE 7010-2020), machine-readable personal privacy requirements (IEEE 7012-2025), and systems that mimic human empathy (IEEE 7014-2024).

IEEE SA offers a suite of ethics in AI programs including IEEE CertifAIEd, which includes two certifications: one for individuals and one for products.

The professional certification provides individuals with skills to assess autonomous and intelligent systems for adherence to IEEE’s methodology and ethics criteria.

“Ethically Aligned Design was the first of its kind in the sense of a report that was more than a list of principles and from an organization with the credibility of IEEE.” —John C. Havens, founding executive director of the global initiative

The product certification program assesses whether an organization’s AI tool or system conforms to the criteria in the IEEE Ontological Specifications. A CertifiAIEd assessor evaluates the product to ensure it meets the criteria. Upon completion of the assessment, the owner submits it to the IEEE Conformity Assessment Program, which can certify it and issue the certification mark.

IEEE SA also recently released its Responsible AI for Children certification program. This program certifies that AI products for children have been independently tested and verified to meet design and ethics requirements.

The IEEE Artificial Intelligence Systems (AIS) for Global Flourishing Collaborative Industry Collections group was formed to provide the resources, knowledge, and tools needed to prioritize people’s well-being to developers of AI systems. It has more than 200 members.

The collaborative describes its work as a practical, systems-level framework for responsible technology design that translates flourishing into applied methods, metrics, and tools that can be used throughout the AIS lifecycle.

The group is establishing guidelines for how to design, evaluate, and govern systems that can measurably improve human well-being and ecological sustainability.

Subcommittees are focused on cognition, education, faith, planetary care, spirituality, and other issues.

“The group is building directly off the well-being chapter of Ethically Aligned Design and the IEEE 7010 standard (Recommended Practice for Assessing the Impact of Autonomous and Intelligent Systems on Human Well-Being),” Havens says. “That’s on purpose because if human and planetary flourishing aren’t addressed first in design, then human well-being and planetary sustainability are afterthoughts.”

To mark the 10-year anniversary, IEEE SA created the five-part “Ethically Aligned Design: 10 Years of Impact” podcast series. It launched on 21 October. New episodes are being released every two weeks.

The episodes center around the vision for EAD, the process for selecting its foundational principles, and how they were translated into the IEEE 7000 standards.

The first podcast, “Origins of Ethically Aligned Design,” explored early efforts to make ethics a practical part of AI and autonomous system design. The experts discussed challenges, debates, global collaboration, and the real-world impact that helped move responsible technology from ethical principles toward actionable standards and practices.

The second podcast, “The Chapters and Committees” focuses on the multidisciplinary groups that worked on turning broad human values into practical guidance for AI and autonomous systems.

The podcast discussions serve as material for a thought leadership program that also includes blog articles about the IEEE 7000 series and educational resources to continue to raise awareness and support of this groundbreaking work.

Kathy Pretz is editor in chief for The Institute, which covers all aspects of IEEE, its members, and the technology they're involved in. She has a bachelor's degree in applied communication from Rider University, in Lawrenceville, N.J., and holds a master's degree in corporate and public communication from Monmouth University, in West Long Branch, N.J.

Gemini 4 Hydrogen Lands Above Argon on the Aggregate Index

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 17 • Originally published at blog.neticslabs.com

Netics visual, 14 November 2026: the four figures a cost decision starts from, all taken from Google's announcement of 12 November 2026.

The interesting part of the launch is what Google chose to publish alongside it. Hydrogen's page carries a ten-row comparison table against Claude Sonnet 5.5 and GPT-6.1 Terra, with the rows where Hydrogen loses left visible. That is unusual in a launch post, and it makes the announcement usable as a routing document.

GitHub's changelog of 13 November 2026, where the model becomes generally available to Copilot Pro, Pro+, Max, Business and Enterprise users at provider list pricing. Page from https://github.blog/changelog/2026-11-13-gemini-4-hydrogen-in-github-copilot/, retrieved 14 November 2026.

The price is $1.50 per million input tokens and $7.50 per million output tokens, and Google states that the price is flat across the whole one-million-token context window. Under Google's flat pricing, a 900,000-token repository dump carries the same unit rate as a 5,000-token question.

Hydrogen also pairs a one-million-token input with up to one million output tokens. Google describes the model synthesising dense PDFs, spreadsheets, charts, audio and recorded video into cited reports and structured deliverables. Consider a hypothetical French or Moroccan SME that scans supplier invoices and contracts: that combination sets the shape of the workflow as one long input, one long structured output and one flat bill.

Google's AutomationBench-AA chart from the 12 November 2026 announcement: score against cost per task, with Hydrogen's four thinking levels plotted as separate points. Image from https://blog.google/innovation-and-ai/models-and-research/gemini-models/introducing-gemini-4-hydrogen/.

Hydrogen ships with four thinking levels: low, medium, high as the default, and xhigh as the new top tier. Google's framing is that the levels scale in a predictable progression, so a team pays for reasoning depth only where a task needs it. On the Artificial Analysis Intelligence Index, the default high level scores 51 and xhigh reaches 54.

That ladder is the practical part of the launch. A slow overnight job can run at xhigh, a chat assistant answering a customer at 10 a.m. can run at low, and both run on one model with one price sheet. The score-versus-time chart Google published shows how the ladder behaves against two competing models: Hydrogen reaches its top score earlier in wall-clock time, and the competing lines sit further to the right.

The index chart from Google's announcement: score against minutes per task. Hydrogen's xhigh point sits near the top of the chart at the shortest time, which is the claim a latency budget should test. Image from https://blog.google/innovation-and-ai/models-and-research/gemini-models/introducing-gemini-4-hydrogen/.

The table inside the announcement is where a careful reader should slow down. Hydrogen wins SWE-sweep at 13.5% against 3.1% for Claude Sonnet 5.5 and 5.8% for GPT-6.1 Terra, and it wins AutomationBench-AA at 76.1% against 71.8% and 60.2%. It loses Terminal-Bench 4.1 at 57.0% against 63.9%, OSWorld-2.1 at 77.6% against 83.9%, and Terminal-Bench-Science 0.1 at 51.4% against 59.9%. On HLE-Diamond it leads at 58.8% against 53.3% and 47.3%.

A single aggregate index collapses those rows into one number, and that number currently places Hydrogen above Argon. The table says something narrower: Hydrogen is the stronger choice for repository-scale bug repair and for automation-style tool work, and the rival model remains stronger for computer-use tasks and for science-heavy terminal work. Any team that reads only the headline will route the wrong task class to the cheaper model and then blame the model.

The comparison table from the 12 November 2026 announcement, reproduced as published, including Terminal-Bench 4.1 (cheating-adjusted) where Claude Sonnet 5.5 leads at 63.9%. Image from https://blog.google/innovation-and-ai/models-and-research/gemini-models/introducing-gemini-4-hydrogen/.

Google also introduced Orchestrator in Google Antigravity, a component that switches between models such as Carbon and Hydrogen inside one session. On Terminal-Bench 4.0, Google reports that Orchestrator reached near-Carbon task success rates while consuming 24% less quota on average and finishing 19% faster. For parallel work, the company describes Carbon acting as the coordinator while Hydrogen powers worker and verifier agents across isolated workspaces.

Read that as the vendor building routing into its own product. Google's own engineering position is that a single model rarely fits a whole session: a hard architecture question wants the expensive reasoning model, and the code edits, test runs and verification that follow want the cheap fast one. A team that already pays for both tiers gains nothing from picking one by habit.

Netics visual, 14 November 2026: the same five rows arranged as a routing decision, where the losing rows sit next to the winning ones.

The pricing detail that carries this is quota accounting. Google measures Orchestrator on quota consumed, so the benefit shows up in a plan's allowance before it shows up in a dollar figure. A team on a flat seat price sees the difference as more work finished per seat.

Four measurements decide whether a cheaper tier belongs in production. The first is cost per finished task, which includes the retries and the failed tool calls that the per-token price hides. The second is latency at the thinking level the team would actually set, measured on the tasks the team actually runs. The third is failure behaviour when a tool call fails or a search returns empty, which Google lists as a specific improvement over Gemini 3.8 Flash. The fourth is where the model is offered, because a model missing from the editor developers use costs more in workflow friction than it saves in tokens.

Google's HLE-Diamond chart from the announcement: score against cost per task. The curve shows why the thinking level, and the cost per finished task it implies, belongs in the routing decision. Image from https://blog.google/innovation-and-ai/models-and-research/gemini-models/introducing-gemini-4-hydrogen/.

Then there is the governance question that arrives with the Copilot rollout. GitHub's changelog states that administrators of Copilot Business and Enterprise manage access through the model policy in Copilot settings, and that under default model enablement a new model is enabled automatically unless an administrator has turned off the global default or disabled that model. An organisation that tracks AI spend per team should check that setting before the picker fills up.

For teams running agents against their own infrastructure, the model choice usually sits inside a larger control plane: the tool permissions, the egress policy and the approval steps around each call. One implementation is a self-hosted AI agent platform, and the model tier is one setting inside it.

Netics visual, 14 November 2026: the measurements that turn a vendor launch into a routing decision, in the order they are worth running.

Hydrogen makes a credible case for the everyday tier of a two-model setup. The evidence that settles the question sits in the ten rows Google published, three of which point at a competitor, and in the cost-per-task curves behind the four thinking levels.

Need sovereign AI infrastructure, cloud cost audits, or automation pipelines? We build and ship them.

Forget Avengers: Doomsday, I want Scarlet Witch to get her own movie that finally does her story justice

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join the GamesRadar community for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Bringing all the latest movie news, features, and reviews to your inbox

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Wanda Maximoff's VisionQuest return proves why now is the perfect time for Scarlet Witch to take the limelight

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Avengers: Doomsday doesn't just mark Doctor Doom's arrival or the Avengers' return, seven long years after Endgame. It's easily the most ambitious endeavour Marvel Studios has ever undertaken, bringing MCU mainstays together with Marvel's First Family, the Fantastic Four, and Fox's original X-Men team.

Avengers: Doomsday is the culmination of storytelling that began at the turn of the century and that's obviously a very big deal, especially for superhero fans who have waited decades to see this epic team-up come to life on the big screen. Still, there's another, far bigger reason why people will be lining up for Doomsday on opening night, and her name is Wanda Maximoff.

Don't take my word for it. Disney reports that last week's VisionQuest finale helped "fuel a spike in ticket sales" for Doomsday while drawing in 3.7 million viewers on Disney+, a series high that represented a 44% jump from the previous episode.

The episode itself was decent enough, a fitting conclusion to Vision's journey, but it was clearly Wanda's return in a surprise post-credits scene that drove this sudden rush for tickets. Watching Doom reawaken Wanda and recruit her in his fight against the Avengers didn't just set up another plot point for Doomsday. It also confirmed that a fan-favourite character was finally set to return, four painstaking years after she appeared to die in Doctor Strange in the Multiverse of Madness.

Vision star Paul Bettany explained in a recent interview how Marvel's head honcho dissuaded the VisionQuest team from bringing Wanda in sooner: "It will just fundamentally all become about WandaVision again," warned Kevin Feige – and he was right to say it. Although not even he could have predicted just how right he was.

Even though the Scarlet Witch didn't return in the main show, only popping up after the story had concluded, it quickly became clear from online responses that Wanda's return did in fact overshadow everything else that came before it (as it should). And that begs the question: If Wanda can generate so much buzz from just opening her eyes in a four-second cameo, imagine what she could accomplish with an entire movie of her own?

There's long been talk of a potential Scarlet Witch film, something which Elizabeth Olsen always seems keen for. Even after it seemed like Wanda might have died in the Doctor Strange sequel, Olsen assumed that this wasn't the end for her beloved character onscreen.

"I'm just making the assumption that they'll have me again," she told Variety in 2022. "I don't know to what capacity, but I hope I'm back. I hope there's also more fun to be had in something different. Where do we go? I feel like we've done so much with her. It's really been a wild couple years with her."

"Wild" is an understatement, not just for Olsen's experience of the character but for longtime fans who have suffered alongside Wanda over the years. Even before she arrived on screen, Marvel always did her dirty, like when they depicted her as a hysterical, unstable woman who erased all mutants in 2005's 'House of M' arc. It was also true again years later when the films followed suit, transforming the Scarlet Witch into a villain overcome with power and emotion.

WandaVision taught us grief is actually love persevering, and boy has our love for Wanda persevered through a lot. Still, it's easy to root for an underdog whose trials eventually shaped her into one of comicdom's most fascinating heroes. It wasn't until her solo turn in WandaVision, however, that all her flaws and complexity were brought to life onscreen, expanding her fanbase to the point where a solo Scarlet Witch film feels not just viable but necessary.

WandaVision taught us grief is actually love persevering, and boy has our love for Wanda persevered through a lot.

In an industry where female-led superhero films remain few and far between, it's about time Olsen was given the chance to spearhead her own movie. It's not like anyone was asking for four Thor films, yet it seems plenty of people are keen to see Wanda in theaters at this point, perhaps more than any other heroine these days.

But if we are going to do this, let's not rehash the crushing grief of WandaVision or the villainous tropes that we're leading into again with Avengers: Doomsday. Fans predict that Wanda's arc moving forward will likely echo the 2010's comic book storyline Children's Crusade where Doctor Doom took advantage of Wanda for his own gain before she was rescued by her kids and set on a path of redemption.

Given how VisionQuest just put the last pieces in place with the introduction of her now-adult son Tommy, it looks more and more likely that Wanda's story in Doomsday and Secret Wars will end with the entire Maximoff family reunited, which feels like the perfect starting off point for a Scarlet Witch movie.

Since her first full appearance in Avengers: Age of Ultron, MCU Wanda has been endlessly traumatised, losing her biological family first as a child and then her chosen family all over again in WandaVision. With so much build-up, a film led by Wanda could finally bring that full-circle, exploring what life looks like for her and the rest of the Maximoff family now that they're reunited at last, all working together as one.

The petition for WandaVision creator Jac Schaeffer to lead the project starts here. She was the one who devised their introduction as a unit in the MCU, centring the family dynamics which would be key to making a continuation of their story work on the big screen.

That, more than the threat they face, is what will be most important when it comes to pulling off a Scarlet Witch film… although the idea of pitting them against Aubrey Plaza's Death, last seen in Agatha All Along, is too enticing to ignore, especially as every member of that family has previously managed to evade her in some shape or form.

Connections to X-Men characters such as Magneto and Quicksilver, often depicted as Wanda's biological family in the comics, could also be explored now that Marvel's Merry Mutants are back on screen (although the MCU may wish to avoid over-complicating things in this fashion for now). Still, the possibilities are endless, which is fitting given that Wanda is a Nexus Being of infinite possibility.

Whatever happens, whether it takes a few years or a few decades, a Scarlet Witch movie feels inevitable at this point. Because why would Marvel refuse to print money? Sure, a film starring Wanda in the lead might have once felt improbable, but the Scarlet Witch has a knack for defying the odds, and there's never been a better time for her to take centre stage than now at the height of her powers, on and off screen.

Avengers: Doomsday arrives on December 16 in the UK and December 18 in the US. For more on the future of the MCU, here are all the upcoming Marvel movies and shows on the way.

Astronomers discover rare black holes that formed when stars failed to go supernova

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Sign up to our monthly entertainment newsletter to keep up with all our coverage of the latest sci-fi and space movies, tv shows, games and books.

Discover this week's must-see night sky events, moon phases, and stunning astrophotos. Sign up for our skywatching newsletter and explore the universe with us!

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

"Black holes are born in some of the most extreme environments in the universe, so the prospect of better understanding their formation is very exciting."

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Gravitational waves have revealed a population of black holes that may have been born when masssive stars failed to go supernova.

Most of us know the origin story of black holes, or we thought we did. Massive stars run out of the fuel for nuclear fusion and their cores collapse. As this happens, the stars' outer layers expand outwards. Shockwaves from the core collapse strike the outer layers, triggering a supernova explosion. The collapsed core creates a black hole.

However, new research based on gravitational wave data collected by LIGO (Laser Interferometer Gravitational-Wave Observatory) and its gravitational wave detector partners, Virgo and KAGRA (Kamioka Gravitational Wave Detector), suggests this might not be the complete picture of black hole birth. The team behind the research suggests some massive stars go out with a whimper rather than a bang and that these "failed supernovas" can also spawn black holes.

The hint of this comes from the over 300 black hole collisions spotted by LIGO-Virgo-KAGRA since the detectors first "heard" ripples in space from these distant events in 2015.

"A vast majority of the merging black holes in the universe are about 10 times the mass of our sun," LIGO team member Katerina Chatziioannou, of the California Institute of Technology (Caltech), said in a statement. "Our data are showing that this group of black holes may have a common origin — they may be a distinct subpopulation that failed to go supernova.

"These results represent a connection between the gravitational-wave data and underlying astrophysics we are trying to extract."

Currently, there is no hard evidence of failed supernovas, but their existence is well supported theoretically. In fact, scientists think there is a vast population of massive stars out there that imploded rather than exploded, directly collapsing into black holes. And these may be black holes with around ten times the mass of the sun (10 solar masses) found in merging pairs.

Chatziioannou led a team that examined gravitational wave data to confirm this concept. To do this, they looked at the distribution of the masses of 257 black hole mergers detected by the LIGO–Virgo–KAGRA network over the past 10 years.

This revealed a peak of 10-solar-mass black holes that represented 75% of the entire sample. If this was expected, what the team found that wasn't predicted was a lack of black holes with masses 12 to 16 times the mass of the sun.

"We see 10 solar-mass black holes merging all the time," team member Isaac Legred of the University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign said. "But why are, say, 14 solar-mass black holes so rare? The gap we see in the distribution of masses indicates there may be a special cocktail of internal physics that goes into forming the abundance of 10 solar-mass black holes."

The researchers also looked at the spin rates of black holes in mergers, a telltale signature of how they formed. This revealed that 10-solar-mass black holes seemed to have a distinct range of spin speeds. This indicates a common origin, perhaps the implosion of a massive star without a supernova explosion.

"We can look up at the sky and try to study historical records of massive stars that failed to explode, but it's challenging because they just vanish without exploding, leaving a dark patch of sky," Legred concluded. "With this study, we show the gravitational-wave data are consistent with most binary black holes forming from a very special kind of failed supernova, but more research is needed.

"Black holes are born in some of the most extreme environments in the universe, so the prospect of better understanding their formation is very exciting."

The team's research was published on Tuesday (Nov. 10) in the journal Physical Review D.

Robert Lea is a science journalist in the U.K. whose articles have been published in Physics World, New Scientist, Astronomy Magazine, All About Space, Newsweek and ZME Science. He also writes about science communication for Elsevier and the European Journal of Physics. Rob holds a bachelor of science degree in physics and astronomy from the U.K.’s Open University. Follow him on Twitter @sciencef1rst.

Imagine a Parker House roll having a tryst with Asian-style milk bread and a classic French croissant—that’s the essence of salt bread, a.k.a. shio pan in Japan and sogeum-ppang in Korea.

“These little buns…they look so cute, but they are so rich,” guest contributor, and our deputy food editor, Hana Asbrink explains to BA Bake Club hosts Jesse Szewczyk and Shilpa Uskokovic. “You can really just snack on them solo and be so satisfied.”

This month’s episode is also more bittersweet than usual, as Shilpa reveals that she is leaving Bon Appétit to take on new projects. “I'm so proud of Bake Club,” says Shilpa. “Thank you to all of our readers and listeners. You really have made this the most wholesome corner of the internet and my career.”

We can’t let her go without a proper send off. Follow Shilpa on Instagram to see what comes next in her baking adventures. And fret not, BA Bake Club will continue full steam ahead with Jesse at the helm!

SU: You've been here with us in spirit in every episode.

JS: This month we are talking about salt bread and for anyone unfamiliar, Hana, do you want to tell us what is salt bread?

HA: Yes. So salt bread, it originated in Japan in, I want to say the mid 2010s and then obviously became popular, got so popular, it hopped over the water. And what it is essentially is a bread bun or dinner roll, if you will, with the qualities of a croissant almost. So the recipe I developed for BA and for Bake Club in particular rests in the middle with the qualities of a more crusty exterior and maintaining that really nice, lovely to eat interior of a milk bread. So just know even though it's called salt bread, you'll find a different range depending on where you go.

SU: And we'll get into why there's a nice crust on the salt bread, which is different from how other crusty breads are achieved. But Hana, you know the drill. We are going to walk through the recipe and for that we're going to need our jaunty Bake Club how-to music. And Hana, walk listeners through the broad strokes of how to make your salt bread.

HA: One of the hallmarks of this salt bread recipe lies in its tangzhong base. So after you create that, it's very straightforward. You're incorporating the wet ingredients, which includes a packet of instant yeast along with the bread flour. And then you are just going to work that dough until it's ready. It's going to get shaped and rested until it doubles in size. And you're going to, in the meantime, cut up some salted butter. This will go in the dough itself. Then comes the fun part, which is the shaping. First, we're going to shape them into balls, then we're going to shape them into cones or carrot shapes. Then we're going to grab a rolling pin and smooth them out, tuck the frozen pieces of butter into each dough and roll them up. They're going to look like mini croissants or crescent rolls. They will rise once more. You're going to spray them with some water, put a little bit of flaky sea salt on top, and then they'll bake in the oven where they will fry and crisp in their own fat because the butter will come out of the little buns. And once they're out, you're going to brush them with this melted butter, let them hang out a little bit so you're not ruining your mouth and then tearing into them.

SU: Okay, Hana, let's talk about the dough a little bit in detail. So you said it starts off with a tangzhong.

HA: Okay, so a tangzhong is a type of roux and you make it just by incorporating starch, in this case, all-purpose flour and water. So it starts off very liquidy.

JS: Sometimes it's like milk too, I know.

HA: Yes, exactly. And you want to cook these two elements together until they become like a gel-like-

HA: Almost snotty consistency like mayonnaise. And you can let it cool a little bit and then it gets incorporated with the rest of the dough's ingredients. And what that does is really help to retain the moisture of a loaf or a bread. If you've eaten milk bread before, you know just how tender-

HA: For days that it can be. And it just really yields that really fluffy consistency.

SU: What is the consistency of the finished dough and how do you get it ... Is it important to really mix it well? Tell us more.

HA: It really is important to mix this well. This is a bread flour-based dough because we want that higher protein level to really give us the structure and the chew that we are aiming for at the end. But the dough itself, it's going to be a beautiful baby-like ball of wonder, just super easy to work with, shiny, smooth, and obviously it's enriched with enough fat in there, so it will be just very resilient and pliable.

SU: Okay. And do you look for a window pane test in this and is it important?

HA: Yes. It's very important here. And I know not everyone has a mixer and I really do try to create recipes that are approachable in a way that everyone can appreciate. So one can potentially just use their hands, but if you do have a mixer or can borrow one for an afternoon, this would be the time to do so because you do need either a dough hook or a strong paddle. It will need about 20 to 25 minutes of pretty aggressive kneading in the bowl. And you'll know it's ready when you just stretch a corner of it. And many of you have heard of this window pane test and you can stretch it thin enough so that it is nearly see through without breaking open and tearing. So if you can manually do this in a respectable amount of time where your arms won't be falling off, then you can achieve this window pane test without tearing, more power to you.

HA: Tim said ... I know Tim is a very active club member.

HA: Yes. I need to see Tim's arm muscles. I have faith in Tim.

SU: And then once the dough is mixed, how long does it rest for before you move on to the next step?

HA: So the dough encounters several resting periods. The first bulk ferment of the dough before it's shaped, that requires a couple of hours. You want it to nearly double in size. And if you want to make this for, let's say the holidays where you're already pressed for time, I've included instructions on how you can get it to a certain level, then stick it in your fridge, let it bulk ferment slowly in that chill zone. But you could potentially just make this salt bread recipe within the course of one day.

JS: So then after that first rise, you divide up the dough and you roll it.

JS: And you don't flour your surface, right?

HA: You do not need any more flour. And I think that's probably a crutch of many home bakers who see, "Oh, my dough is sticky or oh, it doesn't look the way it's supposed to." And the tendency is just to flower your surface, but at the end of doing so, you might create actually a tougher, less tender product. So just trust that there's enough fat in this dough. What you need is just a clean surface and actually you don't even want to put anything on, let's say your counter because that tension is what helps grip and stretch the dough in a very natural way.

JS: There she is. She's in the Bake Club.

HA: Yes, absolutely. So listen, I am the first person to say just measure with your heart, but not when it comes to baking. If you really want to have a good final result and spend all this time making something beautiful that you want to share with loved ones, you really just need to take out an inexpensive scale and measure out roughly ... I think it was 70 grams per ball. You don't have to be so precise across the bun weight. You could be off by maybe five to eight grams, but you should aim for getting somewhere in there so that the bake times can yield the best result for you.

JS: Okay. So you have your dough portions and then what do you do?

HA: You have your dough balls and they are so cute and you're just circling with your claw-like hand to make sure they're around. Then they're going to go under plastic wrap. They're just going to rest for up to 30 minutes and then you will shape them again. Not the last time. You're going to just ... Putting the weight on one side of your hand and roll them into cones or if you want to think about it like a carrot. And by that I mean one end will be a little thicker and then it'll taper down into a cone-like shape. And then it will rest under that same plastic wrap until you're ready to roll out each piece of dough. If you think about an isosceles triangle, a short-

HA: That would be two equal sides. There's equilateral. And who can tell me the third type of triangle? Geometry-

SU: No, that's what she said. Equilateral.

HA: Isosceles and come on, come on. Scalene is each side is a different length.

HA: In isosceles two sides are the same length and equilateral is obviously equal.

JS: Okay. Well, there's 125 grams in a cup of flour.

HA: See, this is the cooking from your heart girl telling you.

JS: Okay, triangle. So we got a triangle of dough.

HA: It's a triangle of dough, two long sides, one short base. Don't forget about the salted butter nubs. So two types-

SU: Because I think this is the crux of the recipe.

SU: Yeah. This is where it gets wild because up to now, Hana, what you're describing really feels like an ordinary enriched dough. Jesse, you and I have spoken about this. It's like a milk bread, you made a tangzhong. Everything feels normal and now is what makes salt bread salt bread.

HA: It's called salt bread, yes, because there's a little flaky salt on top sure. But what this bun is actually about is the butter. So looking at it from the outside, it's so cute. It looks like the cutest, shiniest dinner roll you've ever seen, but it holds a secret. And that secret is salted butter inside. So you take this shape of dough that's not yet baked. You take your chilled piece of salted butter ... And again, there's unsalted butter in the enriched dough, but you're taking a piece of salted butter that I ask you to just keep in the freezer until you're ready to roll. And then you roll it up like you would a mini croissant or a crescent roll. And then you're thinking, okay, now what? So yes, it rests once more, the last rest. And then as I mentioned, we're going to just spray the tops with a water bottle and lay the salt down. They're going to bake and you're going to freak out because ... And also you're going to bake them in an unlined sheet pan, no parchment paper. They're going directly on the hot surface of the sheet pan on the lowest rack of your oven. You really want it to be hot.

HA: You really want it to be hot because one of the other defining features of salt bread is the crusty, dark, burnished bottom. And how does it get burnished? You need all that butter inside each individual roll to leech out directly onto the hot sheet pan. What this does, it effectively fries the underside. It's wild. You're going to think you're doing it all wrong, but again, you just have to trust the process until the buns reach their perfectly golden brown color. And then you're going to remove them and take a fancy Japanese bristle brush [inaudible 00:16:08] and very carefully brush all of that melted butter straight onto your just baked, still hot, porous open buns that are just begging for moisture. You're going to think, oh, this is so wasteful. Don't worry. It's all going to get absorbed on the top, on the bottom. It will be a relatively dry sheet pan by the time you're done with the whole ordeal.

HA: It's brown. You're literally going to think I'm doing this wrong. Just stay with us people because they'll come out, they'll cool, you'll give them a nice butter brushing and you'll be richly rewarded.

SU: I think when you gave us that roll the first time, we were like, holy ... Because you can taste all of these different textures. It's a soft roll. It's a super crusty, crunchy bottom. It's like a potato chip on the bottom.

HA: What did you guys think? Was that your first time trying salt bread?

JS: I've had a weird rendition at this Italian fusion restaurant, so I don't think it was the best. It was nowhere near the star quality of yours.

SU: I've had it once before. It was Justin's salt bread.

SU: It's good, but it's very ... You can feel the grease absorbing into your pores when you eat it. It's really good, but I don't know. It didn't hold the handle to yours, I will say. There was something about the size and the contrast of texture in yours that I thought was really a very successful day.

HA: Thank you so much. Do they serve theirs warm or warmish?

SU: Yes. They warm them up. And then you can also have the option of getting them with an egg salad.

JS: Yeah. I was going to ask, Hana, how do you eat these? Is there anything you pair them with or do you eat them warm?

HA: Oh my gosh. These little buns, they look so cute, but they're so rich. I know people will have a tendency to just grab more butter, and if that is your style, all the power to you. But it's so rich, you can really just snack on them solo and be so satisfied. So remember, they're rolled up into this cute shape. They are two fun ways to eat them. You can unravel them like a little kid might and then expose that initial shape. Or what I like to do, I like to scrunch them because then you can really appreciate their crusty exterior, then tear them in half, and then you need to just admire that hollow that gets created from baking. And it's the hollow that once held that salted butter, that salted tablespoon of butter.

So I did include the link to our Instagram reel, which is basically a one minute long quick tutorial of every important step of this recipe, including the dreaded cone shaping part. And basically what we want to do is ensure that the dough has enough time to rest between each movement, if you will. So the reason why we don't go from ball shape to triangle is because there's a good chance the dough might spring back. So the reason we're giving each phase 30 minutes is just to allow the gluten to be as relaxed as possible because we've all been there where we're stretching out even store-bought pizza dough where we're really trying to will it into a shape and it just wants to pull back and spring back into its six-inch diameter. So what you have to do is essentially just allow for enough time.

Now, if your dough is well rested, if your yeast is fresh, everything should progress smoothly. It almost sounds like maybe your kitchen was warmer than our kitchen and it proofed a lot faster. If they droop down while they're resting, I feel like maybe they're a little over-proofed. What do you think, Jesse? I can't quite tell from the text here.

But also we do recognize that everyone's ovens are different. So I do specify to bake these on the bottom rack because that does tend to be the hottest spot of one's oven, but it looks like maybe your oven is firing on all cylinders and could probably just stand being in the middle rack.

JS: Yeah. So just keep them down. But yeah, to Hana's point, the strange thing that happens with baking equipment where it's the darker the color, typically the hotter it gets, the more it holds onto heat. I guess if you think about a cast iron skillet on a stove top for the same amount of time as a stainless steel skillet, that cast iron's going to hold onto the heat much longer and more intense. And therefore, if you're baking on a dark pan, your bottoms are going to be inevitably darker.

HA: And anecdotally, the darker pans that I've used, they tend to be thinner, like lighter. So they will also just hold and spread that heat much faster than a sturdier, heavier aluminum pan.

JS: Yes. This all checks out and all ovens are different, so you just got to feel it out.

HA: Listen, baking is a fully all sensory experience. So if you smell anything where you're like, that smells a little bit more than caramelized, open the oven. Just check the bottom very quickly, being very careful of course. And you'll soon see if that burnt toast smell is leading you to a darker bottom. And at which point I would just quickly lower the oven, maybe let them hang out off the heat for a few minutes just so the temperatures can recalibrate. But the actual step of the recipe has you bake for the first 10 minutes without bothering them, you're not touching them, you're not doing anything. Then I ask you to open the oven, rotate the baking sheet from front to back and continue baking them until they're really nice and golden on the top. So that would be the perfect time to just very carefully, of course, check the underside of one of the buns. And if they are looking way too dark already, just put them on the middle rack and then keep going. But I would check them not at the full five to eight minute mark, but perhaps at the three-minute mark because your oven just might be a little hotter than ours.

JS: That makes sense. It's like use your baker's intuition.

PlayStation Has No GOTY Nominee at The Game Awards for First Time in 12 Years

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

For the first time in 12 years — since all the way back in 2014 — there is no Sony-published PlayStation title amongst The Game Awards' Game of the Year nominees.

In that time, Sony has won three Game of the Year awards at Geoff's annual show, making it the most successful publisher in the event's history.

But its over-a-decade streak of nominations will finally come to an end here in 2026, as none of its releases from this year have made the cut.

That includes God of War: Sons of Sparta, Marathon, MLB The Show 26, Saros, Marvel Tokon: Fighting Souls, and Marvel's Wolverine.

In particular, Saros and Marvel Tokon got really strong reviews, but given just how stacked this year's competition is, it's no real surprise to see these relatively niche titles be left in the cold.

At the start of the year, many would have probably predicted that Wolverine would be in the mix — but a lukewarm critical reception has ultimately sealed its fate.

Just to recap, this year's Game of the Year nominees are as follows:

Again, since 2015, there hasn't been a single year where a Sony-published title has been ignored. Here's a full list of Sony's nominees in that time:

I don't think anyone would deny that Sony and its PlayStation Studios have had an off year. While their output has actually been quite frequent, none of their latest releases have made a lasting impact in the way that many of the above titles did in their respective years.

Do you think any of Sony's 2026 PS5 games deserved to be nominated for Game of the Year? Claw something out in the comments section below.

Sea Of Stars Is Getting A 'Sunset Edition' Switch 2 Physical And A Gorgeous Final Merch Drop

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sabotage Studio's Sea of Stars is no stranger to a physical release or merch drop, and today, iam8bit has announced that it has one final collection in store.

A brand new Switch 2 physical, the Sea of Stars: Sunset Edition ($49.99), is the star of the show, launching on 2nd April 2027. This one packs the full game and all of its expansions on the cartridge (what a concept!), and comes with some sweet sticker sheets and a download code for the game's soundtrack.

There's a beefier option in the Exclusive Edition, which also boasts an exclusive sleeve and an in-box manual, for those after a little throwback.

The beefiest of the bunch, mind you, is still to come. The Collector's Edition contains all of the goodies outlined above, and throws in a bunch of extra merch too. We're talking a double-sided poster, a tote bag, a reversible bandana, a Rainbow Conch keychain, and seven pins, all housed in a Rainbow Conch Chest. Come on, that's sweet.

The Collector's Edition comes in at a pricey $129.99 (or you can just get the merch for $79.99). All of the above are now available to pre-order from the iam8bit website until 11th January, before shipping in April.

As if all of that wasn't enough, iam8bit has also announced that it has two vinyl LPs in the pipeline, one all about the main game soundtrack, and one featuring the tunes from the Throes of the Watchmaker DLC. You'll also find pre-order information about these on the iam8bit site.

Trezor Warns 27,000 Shipping Orders Were Exposed in Partner Breach, Not Wallet Keys

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

Hardware-wallet maker Trezor has disclosed a breach at shipping partner Skladon affecting data connected with approximately 27,000 customer orders. The incident is serious because a delivery address and product purchase history can reveal who may own crypto. It is also important not to turn a data-exposure story into a false claim that customer wallets were drained. Trezor says its own systems were not compromised, and the disclosed records did not include wallet backups, PINs or passphrases.

Trezor says Skladon informed it on November 15 of unauthorized access linked to an incident on November 7. The data at issue concerned orders placed between August 9 and November 7, 2026. The company estimates roughly 27,000 affected orders and says the investigation is continuing. The records included names, phone numbers, delivery addresses, email addresses and information about orders and products bought. A limited number of earlier August customers may also have been affected even though their information had been anonymized within Trezor’s own systems before the notification process.

Someone with a buyer’s name, address and exact device order can craft a convincing phishing message or impersonate customer support. The same combination may create physical-security concerns if a criminal believes the household controls valuable assets. None of this requires compromising the hardware wallet’s cryptographic security. Trezor says it never requests wallet recovery data by telephone and warns affected users to treat unexpected calls, texts, emails and letters with suspicion. If a message uses authentic order details, that does not make its instructions legitimate.

For related context, see our earlier report on the Unocoin wallet-key incident.

The company says it has contacted affected customers it can still reach and notified a data-protection authority. It is introducing or expanding anonymous pickup at collection points in much of Europe, reducing the amount of residential delivery information involved in future orders. Trezor also says it is working to shorten customer-data retention from 90 to 45 days, with further reductions under consideration. Those steps address the long-term exposure of operational records rather than claiming that a damaged secret key can be repaired after the fact.

Customers should go directly to the vendor’s known website for official support rather than follow contact details supplied in an unsolicited message. They should never type a recovery phrase into a web form or share it with a caller. Buyers can also consider whether their delivery address and household security require extra attention. Anyone receiving threats should contact appropriate local authorities. The precise number of affected people, rather than orders, may differ, and the final breach scope may change as investigators establish what was exported.

A legitimate package or delivery notice is especially convincing because it can refer to a real order. Criminals might use the information for emails, texts, calls or fraudulent web pages that look like shipping support. Wallet owners should be wary of any message that asks them to type a recovery phrase, install a replacement wallet application or approve an unexpected transaction. Those requests are unrelated to normal delivery updates. The strongest precaution is to verify a message through a separately opened official website, not by following a link in the message itself.

For related context, see our earlier report on Coinbase's discussion of cold-storage controls.

The distinction is straightforward: a shipping-partner breach can expose sensitive personal information without exposing the keys securing funds. For users, that does not make the incident harmless; it shifts the immediate risk toward targeted impersonation and privacy rather than a protocol-level wallet failure.

Reporting sources: Trezor official incident report

My Green Condo Inc. Opens Patented Blockchain Accounting Framework (U.S. Patent No. 12,443,952) to SaaS Providers for M&A and Strategic Licensing

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Building on its 2026 blockchain and tokenization roadmap, MGCOne invites legacy property management software vendors to eliminate management-driven churn and acquire a defensible IP moat.

HIGHTSTOWN, N.J., Nov. 17, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- My Green Condo Inc. (MGCOne), the pioneer in association-centric PropTech, today announced it is formalizing strategic licensing, joint venture, and M&A discussions with established accounting and property management SaaS providers to commercialize its foundational intellectual property, U.S. Patent No. 12,443,952.

Following its September industry brief on tokenized real estate, stablecoin settlements, and AI orchestration, My Green Condo Inc. is opening its patented blockchain accounting architecture to incumbent software vendors looking to transition away from aging, vendor-centric database models.

Solving the SaaS Churn and Silo Problem In the traditional community management ecosystem, accounting and ERP software is sold to management companies rather than the Condo, HOA, and Co-op associations themselves. When an association switches management firms, the incumbent accounting provider routinely loses the account, creating structural revenue volatility and costly data migrations.

"Legacy SaaS platforms were built around intermediaries, trapping communities in fragmented databases and leaving software providers exposed to constant contract churn," said Savitha Sathyan, Co-Founder and Head of Technology at My Green Condo Inc. "With our patent, we hold the protected architectural blueprint for on-chain association accounting and instant estoppel verification. Rather than forcing incumbents to spend years on foundational R&D, we are offering established SaaS leaders an immediate path to license or acquire this IP and lead the industry's next era."

Strategic Advantages for SaaS Acquirers and Licensees:

My Green Condo Inc. is actively engaging corporate development teams and software executives to structure bilateral licensing agreements or strategic acquisitions.

My Green Condo Inc. is a PropTech company delivering end-to-end community management solutions for Condo, HOA, and Co-op associations. Founded by former Wall Street bankers and seasoned association board members, the company is dedicated to modernizing property management.

The company's flagship platform, MGCOne, is the industry's first patented, association-centered operating system. Engineered to elevate community living and modernize governance, MGCOne empowers property managers, board members, and residents through advanced automation and seamless communication. The platform grants communities' absolute control over their data, financial ledgers, and operational continuity.

Leveraging advanced blockchain infrastructure and next-generation AI, MGCOne centralizes operations into an ultra-secure "System of Record." Inspired by the Amazon marketplace model, it delivers unprecedented transparency, robust security, and independence for residents, board members, and service providers alike.

For more information, visit www.mygreencondo.net.

NPM Workspaces: Why Does My Monorepo Build Work Locally but Break in CI?

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

It's 6:40 pm. Your pull request is green on your laptop. You push, CI goes red, and the error says Cannot find module '@acme/core'. You didn't touch that import. Nobody did. You re-run the pipeline anyway, because hope is a strategy, and it fails again.

If you've got two or three packages sharing code in one repo, you've probably met some version of this. NPM workspaces are the built-in way to keep those packages consistent: one install, one lockfile, shared dependency versions, and one command that runs a script everywhere. But the feature has sharp edges that most tutorials skip.

Here's the open question for this article: why did the build pass on your machine and fail on a clean checkout? We'll answer it before the end.

If you like practical monorepo write-ups like this, follow me here on Medium. I post one every week.

By the end you'll know how to set up workspaces, run scripts recursively, filter to the packages you care about, fake parallel runs (npm doesn't do them natively), and avoid the hoisting trap that hides missing dependencies.

Workspaces let one root package.json manage several packages in the same repository. They landed in npm 7, so any modern Node install has them. Check yours:

Before you rely on any flag in this article, skim the official npm workspaces docs for your version. The CLI is still moving.

Before we get into the examples, a quick note: The code snippets provided here are meant purely for understanding the concept. Always refer to the official documentation for the most current API and syntax.

We'll build one small repo and grow it as we go. Two packages: @acme/core (a tiny shared library) and @acme/app (a consumer). Both are TypeScript.

(Strip the // comment line if you paste these into real JSON files. I'm using it as the file name label.)

Two things matter here. "private": true stops you from accidentally publishing the root. And TypeScript is installed once, at the root, so every package uses the same compiler version. That's the "make things the same" part: one version of the tool, everywhere.

Those are symlinks. Your workspace packages show up in the root node_modules as links to the real folders, so import "@acme/core" resolves to your local source instead of the registry. Edit core, and the app sees it. No npm link dance.

Real-world version. Notice the "*" range for @acme/core. npm sees that a local workspace satisfies it and links the folder instead of fetching anything. If you pin a version the workspace doesn't match, say ^2.0.0 while core is at 1.0.0, npm will go to the registry and install a copy from there. That's a quiet source of "why is my local change ignored" bugs.

Under the hood. There's a single package-lock.json at the root. Workspaces are treated as link nodes in one dependency tree, which npm's tree builder (Arborist) dedupes and hoists to the root node_modules where it can. One tree, one lockfile, one answer to "which version of this is installed?"

So far everything looks smooth. What happens when you run the build for the whole repo?

The answer to "run this script in every package" is the --workspaces flag, or -ws for short.

npm walks each workspace and runs its build script. If some packages lack the script, add --if-present so npm skips them instead of failing:

Look at that order. App built before core. Keep that in mind.

Here's the trap. On your laptop, you already built core yesterday, so packages/core/dist exists. App's tsc finds the old dist/index.d.ts, compiles happily, and the whole thing looks fine.

On a clean CI checkout, dist doesn't exist. App builds first (workspaces from a glob like packages/* typically resolve alphabetically, and app comes before core), and TypeScript can't find the types:

That's the answer to our open question. The recursive flag runs workspaces in the order npm lists them. It does not read your dependency graph. Locally, a stale dist folder was covering for the missing ordering.

The fix is to be explicit about order in the root script:

Now clean checkouts and warm laptops behave the same. To prove it to yourself, delete both dist folders and run npm run build from the root. It should succeed every time.

Have you ever had a build that only worked because of leftover output on your disk? Hold that thought, there's a cousin of it later in the article.

Under the hood. --workspaces is a config switch that tells the command to run in the context of all configured workspaces. Commands that touch the dependency tree (install, update) link workspaces into node_modules. Commands that run things (test, exec, publish) go to the root project unless you pass a workspace flag. That's why a bare npm test at the root doesn't run your packages' tests. It runs the root's.

The root script solved ordering, but typing -w @acme/core all day gets old. Is there a better way to aim at one package?

NPM doesn't have a separate "filter" command. The -w (or --workspace) flag is the filter.

All three target the same package. The docs say valid values are a workspace name, a path to a workspace directory, or a path to a parent directory, which selects every workspace inside it. The short folder name works in practice too, but I'd use the package name in scripts because it survives folder renames.

Adding dependencies is where -w earns its keep:

The dependency lands in core's package.json, and the root lockfile updates. You never hand-edit the wrong file again.

Want to include the root project in a workspace run? Use --include-workspace-root:

And the escape hatch: --workspaces=false tells npm to ignore workspaces for a command, which is handy when a config file sets them on by default.

For smarter filtering, use npm query. It takes CSS-like selectors:

Both print a JSON array of matching packages. It's a good way to feed names into a script, though I'd keep it for CI tooling and not daily use.

Under the hood. -w doesn't change which tree gets built. The whole repo still has one dependency tree. The flag only narrows which packages the command runs against. That's also why installing with -w can still touch the shared lockfile and hoisted node_modules.

Here's a quick mid-article check: do you target workspaces by name or by folder in your own scripts? Tell me which and why in the comments. I'm curious whether folder paths are still winning out there.

Filtering gets one package. What about running several at the same time?

Not by themselves. --workspaces goes one at a time. Anyone claiming otherwise is describing a different tool.

For long-running tasks like watch mode, add concurrently at the root:

Run npm run dev and you'll see interleaved, labeled output like:

Parallel is for independent work, like watchers and linters. Do not run builds in parallel when one package consumes another's output. That's the build-order bug again, but now it's flaky instead of consistent, which is worse. Flaky bugs eat evenings.

My rule: sequential && for builds, parallel for watchers.

Under the hood. concurrently just spawns child processes and prefixes their output. Each npm run ... -w call is its own npm process doing its own tree lookup. That's fine for a handful of packages. If you find yourself orchestrating dozens with caching and a task graph, that's the point to look at a dedicated build tool, such as Turborepo or Nx, and keep npm workspaces underneath for installs. Workspaces handle installs and linking. They aren't a task runner, and that's okay.

Next up is the trap I teased earlier, the sibling of the stale dist folder.

Because npm hoists shared dependencies to the root node_modules, a package can import something it never declared and still work.

Say core uses dayjs and declares it. App imports it too, but forgot to add it to its own package.json:

It works, because dayjs sits in the root node_modules where app's Node resolution finds it. The bug is invisible until someone publishes @acme/app on its own, or moves it to another repo, and it explodes with "module not found."

The fix is boring and correct: declare what you import.

Now both packages list dayjs themselves. npm still stores one copy at the root if the versions agree, so you keep the disk savings and the honesty.

My opinion: this is the number one thing most developers get wrong with workspaces. They treat the root node_modules like a shared bucket. It's a cache of the tree, not a permission slip.

Under the hood. Hoisting is a dedupe optimization in the tree builder, and Node's resolution walks up parent folders looking for node_modules. The two together create "phantom dependencies." Stricter package managers fight this by design. With npm you have to enforce the discipline yourself, for example through a lint rule or a quick review habit.

Last stretch. Workspaces have more commands that keep your packages consistent with each other.

Most npm commands accept -w or -ws. Four are worth knowing.

Scaffold a new package. npm init -w creates the folder if it doesn't exist and adds it to the root workspaces setup:

Edit package.json fields everywhere. This is my favorite consistency tool:

The first sets the same Node engine range in every package. The second prints each package's version, keyed by name.

Here's the full running example one more time, the root file you should end up with:

I kept typecheck out of the earlier sections on purpose. Try it, and think about why --noEmit against app would fail on a clean checkout (hint: same cause as before, no core types without a core build). That's your homework.

Remember 6:40 pm and the missing @acme/core? Your laptop had a stale dist folder quietly doing the job that build ordering should have done. CI had no such luxury. Workspaces gave you linking, one lockfile, and one install. They did not give you a dependency-aware build order, and they never promised to.

Further reading from folks who've covered this ground: Faiz Ahmed on npm workspaces, a gentle intro from Wasp, Safeguard's dependency walkthrough, the Publishing Project's take, and Paul Serban's guide. If a teammate keeps fighting this exact monorepo build bug, send them this article.

Did this approach match how you are solving it, or do you have a different take? Drop a comment. I genuinely read every single one.

If this saved you even a few minutes of debugging or confusion, hit that clap button so others can find it too. It really does make a difference.
