Branch isolation in a live Supabase app

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 17 • Originally published at syedmuhammadhussain.pages.dev

To keep branches apart in one Supabase database, give every branch-owned table a region_id, fill it in for existing rows, make it NOT NULL, stamp it on the server, and let Row Level Security decide which rows each signed-in user gets back. Filtering in React isn't enough, because the browser holds a public key and can ask the database for anything. Check the result with row counts, because RLS hides rows without raising an error.

I built Zarrar Cooling Services (ZCS) on my own: a React and Supabase platform for an appliance repair and cooling business, with a public booking site, an admin panel, a technician panel, and branch views. Version 1 served one branch, Islamabad (which also covers Rawalpindi). Then the business opened a branch in Jhelum.

The requirement sounded simple. Staff in one branch must not see another branch's jobs, payroll, invoices, or stock. The owner must see everything. And the app was live, with real bookings coming in, so I couldn't rebuild it from an empty database.

Most Supabase guides start from an empty database. This one starts from 24 tables of real data: the model I chose, the migration in the order I ran it, the policies, and what I have and haven't tested.

So anyone can copy the publishable key from DevTools and query the Data API directly. A region dropdown in React is a convenience; only a policy in Postgres keeps Jhelum's payroll away from an Islamabad technician. In ZCS the /admin routes only checked that you were signed in, so RLS was already the real authorisation layer.

This isn't a theoretical risk. UpGuard found 16,326 Supabase databases with publicly readable tables among about 300,000 domains showing signs of Supabase use, and more than half had indicators of personal data (UpGuard, "Everything, everywhere: systemic data exposure in Supabase apps"; the page is dated 14 Oct 2026, and BleepingComputer covered it on 28 Sep 2026). BleepingComputer summarises the causes as "missing or ineffective row-level security policies and misuse of public keys." OWASP ranks broken access control first in its 2025 Top 10 and says access control "is only effective when implemented in trusted server-side code" (OWASP A01:2025).

A tenant is a separate customer organisation sharing your infrastructure. Tenants must never see each other's data, and nobody on the tenant side sees across tenants. ZCS isn't that. It's one company with several branches, which I'll call regions because that's the name in the schema.

Multi-tenant SaaS compared with ZCS regions

What carries over is the mechanism: an ID column on every owned row, a table linking users to the IDs they may access, a SECURITY DEFINER helper for that lookup, and policies that use it. For real multi-tenancy the migration steps below still apply, but the trust model usually needs to be stricter, for example no cross-tenant super-users inside the app.

Before writing any SQL, I compared five options:

The deciding argument was "existing hooks keep working": my plan estimated that about 14 pages would need no code changes, because the same select simply returns fewer rows.

Regions are data, not an enum, so a new branch never needs a migration:

24 tables are region-owned and get a region_id: work orders, invoices, expenses, staff, inventory, suppliers, purchase orders, assets, the payroll tables, notes, reports, activity logs, and invoice settings. Child tables such as attachments and invoice items take the region of their parent. Shared tables (services, system settings) stay global. Customers are a special case: the table has no region_id, and any branch can add to it, but a policy only shows a customer to a branch that has a work order for them.

One function answers "may the current user touch this region?":

Real ZCS helper (migration 20260906101730)

It's SECURITY DEFINER (it runs with its owner's rights) so it can read user_regions and super_admins without triggering their own RLS. A policy that queries another RLS-protected table can recurse into itself. ZCS had already been bitten by that on user_roles and fixed it with the same pattern, which is the standard answer in the Supabase community.

Figure: a request carries the user's JWT; Postgres runs the policy, which asks the helper whether this user may access the row's region. For super-admins, the region switcher narrows what the database returns, because the helper reads user_active_region. That's a scope they choose, not a security boundary. For everyone else, it's only a badge.

I never migrated the database the website was using. I made a copy of it as a new Supabase project and ran every migration below on that copy. I tested the updated app against it on my computer, and ran the same changes on a local Supabase stack.

When everything passed, I put the site into maintenance mode during off-hours, so nothing new was being written. I copied across the data the live database had gained since the copy was made, then changed the production environment variables so the website points to the new project. The original database is untouched and kept as a backup.

The site was in maintenance mode during the switch, and I didn't time it, so I'm not claiming zero downtime.

The first step wasn't SQL that changed anything. I counted every row in the 24 region-owned tables as the postgres role, which bypasses RLS: a little under 300 rows in total, plus the customers and auth users. It's a small business; the method matters more than the size. Every later phase had to leave those numbers unchanged. Under RLS, a denied read returns an empty result with no error, so a broken policy looks exactly like an empty table, and "the page loads" proves nothing.

All of this ran on 6 September 2026. Each phase was its own migration file, created with supabase migration new, applied through the SQL editor, and written with a ROLLBACK block in its header.

The migration phases, all run on 6 September 2026

Adding a nullable column with no default doesn't rewrite the table in PostgreSQL (PostgreSQL: ALTER TABLE). It still takes a short exclusive lock, which is harmless at this size and something to plan for on large tables.

The backfill put every row in Islamabad, even jobs that were really Jhelum work; those moved later, one at a time, through a "Move to region" action. A uniform backfill kept every check to one question: is the total still the same?

Phase 3, abridged (migration 20260906120956).

Phase 5 is where the column became enforced. The trigger matters as much as the constraint:

Phase 5, abridged (migration 20260906122629)

The browser never chooses a region: on insert the trigger overwrites whatever the client sent, and only trusted server-side functions (public booking, region provisioning) pass a region through a transaction-local setting. On update, region_id is immutable for everyone except super-admins.

The riskiest part was the policies, so I did them in two moves. In phases 6a–6d, I added a new _rgn_ policy for every operation on every table, written in its final form: the existing role condition AND can_access_region(region_id). Because Postgres combines permissive policies with OR (PostgreSQL: Row Security Policies), the old policies still granted the same access, so nothing changed for users. Work orders went last, because every page touches them.

Then a single migration dropped the old policies, keeping a short list of deliberate exceptions: the public booking insert, staff reading their own record, and the personal notifications inbox. A count check in the step before had already flagged a real problem: every inventory transaction had no location, so none of them had a region, and the new policy would have hidden all of them. So the tightening migration first made that policy accept location-less rows, then dropped the old ones. Before applying it, I ran it inside a transaction as the owner and rolled it back. The dry run showed the expected counts, and I applied it for real.

Opening Jhelum on 7–8 September turned up three more issues. The first two came from the row-count check, not from clicking around:

Every region-owned table follows one pattern. This is the work orders UPDATE policy, slightly simplified:

work_orders update policy (slightly simplified)

Authorisation never comes from a value the client sends. The region comes from auth.uid(), the user ID inside the JWT that Supabase has already verified, looked up against user_regions on the server.

Child tables follow their parent through a second SECURITY DEFINER helper, region_of_work_order(id), so an attachment is visible only if its work order's region is.

What I'd change (recommended, not yet done):

PostgreSQL is explicit: "Referential integrity checks, such as unique or primary key constraints and foreign key references, always bypass row security" (PostgreSQL: Row Security Policies).

In practice, if invoices.work_order_id is a plain foreign key, a user in region A who knows the ID of a region B work order can create an invoice in region A that points at it. RLS checks the invoice's own region_id, which is A, so the insert passes. The foreign key check only asks "does this work order exist?", without RLS.

Status in ZCS: child tables inherit their parent's region through policy joins, but the foreign keys between region-owned tables are still plain. Same-region composite foreign keys were deferred during the migration. UUIDs make guessing impractical, but IDs leak through URLs and exports, so I treat this as an open gap. I haven't tested it on the real app.

The fix is to make the foreign key carry the region:

In the companion demo (plain PostgreSQL 16 with a Supabase-style mock, not a real Supabase stack), swapping this back to a plain foreign key made the isolation test fail on exactly this case.

Done, before the switch: on a local Supabase stack and on the migrated copy, I worked through every table, form and field by hand, including a newly created region. I didn't keep a written checklist of those runs, so I'm not listing individual results.

Done, on the migrated project (now the live one):

Not done yet: a two-region test where an Islamabad user and a Jhelum user each try to read, insert, update, delete, move and cross-link the other's data. That's the next test I'd add:

The companion demo does this on a simplified four-table schema: eight checks, including a super-admin moving a work order and its invoice following along. All eight pass. I also planted one leak per run to show the test can fail. A plain foreign key, an extra using (true) policy, a region helper that always says yes, and an anon grant were each caught. Making the stamping trigger a no-op did not fail the test, because WITH CHECK blocked the cross-region writes on its own. That's a useful result in itself: the trigger keeps data correctly filed; RLS is the security boundary.

Adding branches to a live Supabase app came down to a small number of database rules, applied in an order where each step could be checked and undone. The SQL was the easy part. The practical takeaway: write down your row counts before you start, and treat any count that changes unexpectedly as a stop sign, because under RLS a mistake doesn't throw an error. It just shows less data.

Is the Supabase publishable (anon) key safe to put in the browser? Yes, it's designed to be public, but only because Row Level Security decides what it can reach. With RLS off or a policy missing, anyone holding the key can read that table through the Data API. The secret (service_role) key bypasses RLS and must stay on the server.

Is region or branch isolation the same as multi-tenancy? No. Multi-tenancy separates different organisations that must never see each other's data. Branch isolation separates parts of one company, often with super-users who see everything and some shared data. Both use the same tools: an ID column, a membership table and RLS policies.

Do foreign keys bypass RLS in Supabase? Yes. PostgreSQL runs foreign key checks without applying row security, so a row can reference another branch's row if its ID is known. Including the branch column in the foreign key, (region_id, parent_id) → parent(region_id, id), keeps references inside one branch.

How do I test Supabase RLS policies? Act as a real user by switching to the authenticated role with that user's JWT claims, never as postgres or service_role. Try to read, insert, update, delete and link another branch's rows, and assert on row counts, because RLS hides rows without raising errors. Also confirm each user still sees their own data.

Can I add a region_id column to a live Supabase table safely? Adding a nullable column with no default only changes PostgreSQL's catalogue and doesn't rewrite the table, though it briefly takes an exclusive lock. Backfill existing rows next, then add NOT NULL, the foreign key and an index, which all validate the data and fail if anything was missed. Rehearse on a copy first and compare row counts before and after.

Code: the foreign key example comes from a simplified companion demo (four tables, not the ZCS schema), run on plain PostgreSQL with a Supabase-style mock.

AI note: I built ZCS with AI coding agents, mainly Claude Code, alongside Cursor and Antigravity. I made the design decisions, ran each migration and checked it with the counts described here.

Build a Country Allowlist in Cloudflare Workers and Nginx

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 17 • Originally published at networkdatalabs.com

Product said the app should only be available in the United States and Canada. The first instinct was to drop a country database onto every origin and start returning 403s. Then we remembered the Worker already has a country code on the request, for free, with no extra lookup.

A country gate is a coarse filter: it looks at the IP’s country, not at the person. Behind a known VPN network, a Tor exit or a privacy relay, the country you see is the egress, not the user’s. location.country_code can also be null, so every example below fails open when the country is unknown or the lookup errors.

Use the ISO 3166-1 alpha-2 code (US, CA, …), not the city. City-level data is a best-effort estimate; save it for soft decisions like defaults or language, not a hard 403.

This post starts with request.cf.country, then shows when to look the IP up so Nginx can enforce the same rule. The longer version is the guide to blocking countries by IP in Workers and Nginx.

For the current visitor, Workers already give you a country code on request.cf.country. If all you need is “allow the United States and Canada, otherwise 403”, use it. That is the right default, and it does not spend API quota.

Reach for GET /v0/ip/{ip} when Nginx at the origin has to enforce the same policy (there is no request.cf there), when you want one source of truth for Workers and origin, or when you’re classifying IPs that are not the current visitor (logs, webhooks). Country is part of geolocation, which is on the Free plan: 1,000 requests per UTC calendar month, 1 request/second, for personal and non-commercial use. Cache per IP or you’ll burn the quota on repeat visitors. Commercial use needs a paid plan; paid plans start at $9/month.

The country code, name, continent and is_eu fields are documented on the IP to Country API page.

Store the key with npx wrangler secret put NDL_API_KEY, not in wrangler.toml. The visitor IP is in CF-Connecting-IP. Cache the JSON per IP in the Cache API, keep a tight timeout and fall back to request.cf.country (or allow) if the call fails:

The Cache API is per data center and costs nothing extra. If you want one shared cache across locations, store the JSON in Workers KV with expirationTtl instead. Use request.cf.country when you only need the current visitor’s country at the edge; use the API when origin must apply the same rule, or when you’re looking up IPs that aren’t this request. The two can occasionally disagree, so pick one source for the gate and stick to it.

Nginx doesn’t have the visitor country on its own unless you add a geo module or a local database. A small sidecar keeps the policy in one place: Nginx asks it on each request via auth_request, and the sidecar looks up GET /v0/ip/{ip}, caches the result and returns 200 (allow) or 403 (deny). The sidecar must fail open too: API errors return 200, and if the sidecar itself is down, Nginx maps 502/503/504 to 200.

Pass a single client IP in X-Client-IP. If Nginx is at the edge, that’s $remote_addr. If Cloudflare is the only proxy in front of Nginx, use $http_cf_connecting_ip. Don’t parse raw X-Forwarded-For — the client can put any address there.

auth_request_set copies X-IP-Country from the sidecar onto $ip_country, so the origin still sees the country after an allow. Skip the gate on health checks and static assets if you can, so those paths never spend a lookup (the in-process cache already covers repeat IPs).

Cache per IP for a few hours in the sidecar and in the Worker. Keep timeouts tight (500–800 ms) and always fail open on errors. Country is on the Free plan, but 1,000 requests/month is not enough to look up every request without a cache. For log backfill, POST /v0/bulk (paid plans) accepts up to 100 IPs per request; each IP counts toward the monthly quota.

If the user is on a VPN, you get the egress country. is_vpn (known VPN networks), is_tor and is_relay (privacy relay) are paid-plan flags on the same response if you want to treat that traffic differently. They are not required for a basic country gate.

Google’s Spark AI agent becomes ‘Tasks’ — and now it’s free

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Tasks in Gemini allows the AI agent to call businesses on your behalf, purchase items with Google Pay, and more.

Tasks in Gemini allows the AI agent to call businesses on your behalf, purchase items with Google Pay, and more.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Google is rebranding its Spark AI agent as “Tasks” and making it free to use. The feature, which is powered by Google’s new Gemini 4 Hydrogen AI model, can do things like create meal plans, manage your calendar, and dig through your emails to find recurring expenses you might not need.

Users in the US will get access to a few other features, including the ability for the AI agent to call businesses to compare product pricing and check inventory, purchase items with Google Pay, and sign into accounts that support Google Password Manager. US users can also assign Gemini tasks by emailing gemini@google.com or texting it.

Tasks in Gemini is just one of the many agentic AI features that have emerged over the past year, including Claude Duet, OpenAI’s Dots, and Meta’s Muse. Google says Gemini will perform tasks in a “hardware-isolated virtual machine that’s dedicated to your account and never shared with other users.” You can also provide instructions for Gemini while it completes tasks. When performing more sensitive tasks, like purchasing an item, it will stop working and ask for your permission before following through.
